the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the time-zone rules are applied in a uniform manner. but i say only 'relatively', because the reality is that studies and complaints show that, unfortunately, there is often a complete lack of transparency in the operation, allocation and development of the use of these time slots, but that, moreover, the interpretation of the current rules by the different states of the union has not been uniform, much less. on this basis, the commission decided on 20 june 2001 to review the rules and to take a two-stage approach. i have to say that all this is also in line with what has been said and reiterated here in this parliament on various occasions. the first of these aims to clarify the various provisions of the regulation which, when applied, have revealed shortcomings and led to different interpretations in the various member states. but it is also a question of solving a number of technical deficiencies which have been pointed out to us by the airlines themselves and by the airports and the various operators. finally, several members have pointed out something essential: the need to define what a time slot is and we clearly choose to define it as a right of use. but let us know that this is very important if we are to obtain legal certainty and get out of the present situation where there is nothing, no concrete definition of the nature of the time zones. because how are we going to establish a property right, your honours? there are time zones for takeoff, for landing, but also for flying over. there are no property rights, what there are are are rights of use over a public good which is the airspace, which are the possibilities of take-off or landing outside of what are the facilities of airports or the areas to be crossed depending on the supports and aids to air traffic that are supplied from different places. if this is the first stage, which is essentially technical - and i thank mr deva for saying that we have achieved a good balance, i think that is what we are doing - the second stage is the review of the possibilities for allocating time slots by introducing transparent, non-discriminatory market mechanisms. of course, we will do so carefully, with caution, to avoid in any case excessive or unbearable shocks to our air sector. we want to consolidate our european air transport sector, and we do not want to weaken it. but, pending that second proposal - for which we are preparing studies to justify and guarantee that such a clash does not occur - the present situation must be clear. with this approach in mind, the proposal aims to ensure maximum efficiency in the use of scarce resources such as time slots and to ensure uniformity in the interpretation of the rule and the application of the regulation and therefore legal certainty. to this end, the proposal introduces effective and transparent procedures and a system of penalties which make it possible to achieve the maximum degree of effectiveness without, therefore, altering the system of acquired rights. i would like to thank this parliament, and especially mr stockmann, for the work they have done - always with a constructive approach - and for their dedication. we particularly appreciate the thoughtful and pragmatic approach: we believe that, of the 57 amendments submitted, most of them (35 in total) add clarity to the proposal and give it greater effectiveness and therefore the commission can accept them, with amendments in some cases. on the contrary, we reject the other 22, which i shall briefly mention. firstly, amendments nos 53, 54 and 55, which could significantly affect the balance of the proposal, touching on essential points such as the prohibition of creating a black market through false exchanges, as well as those concerning sanctions. the commission has not adopted these amendments and the commission of course rejects them. secondly, amendments nos 1, 7, 18 and 28, which would significantly change the balanced objectives of the proposal we are making today. amendment no 8 exempts the coordinator from all responsibility, despite his increased responsibilities and capacities, and therefore cannot count on the commission's support. we could, however, accept amendment no 48 with amendments to the wording to ensure that coordinators are liable in the event of gross negligence or wilful misconduct. amendment no 20 is incompatible with the definition of the time zone and ignores the need for a capacity analysis to be carried out at all times before imposing the coordination of the time zones. it must therefore also be rejected. the commission considers amendment no 23 to be too broad and does not share it; the same applies to amendment no 25, which ignores the rules of procedure, which are an operational requirement for the coordination committee if transparency is to be ensured. therefore, both are rejected. similarly, amendments nos 33 and 34 except for the reference to community guidelines. amendments nos 35 and 43 must also be rejected, since they have a negative impact on the community law already recognised on intermodality and public service obligations. i would add that amendment no 57, to which mr pohjamo has referred earlier, introduces a deadline which is impossible to meet. the commission has just launched the procedure for selecting an expert to deliver its report, and we will have it by the summer of 2003 at the earliest. the commission has undertaken to consult the air transport sector and the member states on the results of a study on the market system for the allocation of slots before making a new legislative proposal on this issue. finally, we consider amendments nos 2, 15, 26, 38, 45, 46 and 50 to be either redundant or inconsistent with the world practice of allocating bands and therefore we cannot accept them. i would like to point out, ladies and gentlemen, that i agree with mr jarzembowski on the urgency of the matter. i hope the council will be able to take a decision at the next meeting, although i think that will be difficult, given the progress of technical work. in any case, i want to thank you for your contributions and your work, with a very special mention for mr stockmann, for his magnificent work. i hope that tomorrow you will vote in favour of this commission proposal.
the commission is not prepared to accept this. - mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i believe that we are today concluding the parliamentary procedure for the establishment of the european maritime safety agency, considering that the three parties involved, parliament, the council and the commission, have worked well together with the common objective of completing the procedure as quickly as possible. they deserve congratulations on all the executives who took part. i would remind you that at the end of its first reading last june, parliament adopted 17 amendments, aimed in particular at strengthening the agency's independence from the commission, strengthening the powers of its administrative board, introducing the possibility for member states to request technical assistance from the agency in certain cases and including a regulation to combat fraud. as regards the composition of the administrative council, parliament rejected the participation of its representatives and considered their experience in maritime safety matters as a criterion for appointing members. finally, it asked for the relevant evaluation study of the agency's activities to be carried out by external experts. in its common position, the council accepted the european parliament's amendments providing for the agency to act on assistance to candidate countries without an explicit request from the commission, the agency's obligation to forward the findings of visits to the member states not only to the commission but also to the member state concerned, the commission's sole advisory role in the adoption of the agency's work programme by the management board, the obligation to appoint the members of the management board on the basis of their level of experience in the areas covered by the agency, the obligation of the executive director to respond to requests for assistance from the member state, the appointment or removal of the executive director by the management board, while the commission may nominate its own candidates, without any obligation on the part of the commission to nominate one of them. some of parliament's amendments were incorporated in part into the common position, such as the provision concerning the composition of the agency's management board, on which parliament decided not to be represented and which will ultimately be composed of one representative from each member state, four representatives of the commission and four professionals appointed by the commission. also in the provision referring to the work programme preparation process, where the council specified that the executive director would submit the work programme to the management board after consulting the commission and not only after its prior approval. this also applies to most of the provisions relating to the agency's budget and to the provisions relating to the evaluation of the agency within five years of its establishment. among the new elements introduced by the council in the proposal for a regulation are: the recasting of article 2 on the agency's tasks which, without altering the substance of the content, makes them clearer; the reference to transparency and the protection of information; the omission of the reference to the seat of the agency - i will mention it, however -; the provision for a special procedure for the approval of the work programme in the event of disagreement with the commission; the representation of all member states on the management board and the participation of non-vocational professional representatives without the right to vote; the possibility for the management board to examine specific matters without the presence of professional members; the appointment of the executive director by a qualified majority of 4/5 of the members. in view of the above, it appears that the council took account, in whole or in part, of a significant number of parliament's amendments when formulating its common position and that the spirit and objective of the original proposal, namely to tackle problems of safety at sea and protection of the marine environment, is generally maintained. the following points of the common position should nevertheless be noted: the representatives of the professions are excluded and professionals nominated by the commission are proposed, who have no voting rights and are excluded from meetings when appropriate. a specific procedure shall be introduced for the approval of the agency's work programme in the event of disagreement by the commission. in order to ensure balances between the two institutions, the common position requires the adoption of the programme in this specific case, either by a two-thirds majority of the members, including the commission representatives, or by unanimity of the representatives of the member states. this ensures a balance between the council and the commission, even if the decision-making process becomes more difficult. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a common position on the proposal for a directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the protection of employees in the event of the insolvency of their employer. we believe that the proposal should be adopted without delay. finally, as regards the seat of the proposed organisation, i would like it to be in a city with a large, old port, close to a modern airport with direct flights to all the countries of the european union, with all the necessary infrastructure, in an island country with a maritime history of thousands of years, with the first merchant fleet in the world. obviously, i mean luxembourg...
- mr president, i shall simply try to develop a point which i consider to be important. this point concerns the possibility of testing certain products and of animal experiments when alternative methods are not available. in other words, we should be careful to vote for those amendments which allow the use of alternative methods, particularly in cases of toxicity and reproduction where there are currently no satisfactory alternative tests. i am also in full agreement with the rapporteur that we should finally, when there are possibilities for using alternative methods to avoid the use of animals, use these in vitro alternative methods. the amendment which i have tabled with other colleagues therefore seeks to allow the use of alternative methods, but not in cases where they cannot replace animals. i should like to stress that the priority is human health and, of course, respect for and protection of animals. i would like to believe that parliament will vote in favour of these amendments, which give this possibility until these tests are replaced by alternative in vitro methods. i would like to say that it is superficial and unreasonable to exclude any possibility when at the moment this possibility is not covered by alternative methods.
commission. – mr president, on behalf of the commission i would like to thank mrs roth-behrendt and the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy for this report and congratulate them on the impressive work they have done. the wish expressed by parliament in its opinion for the first reading to improve consumer protection has, to a large extent, been taken into account in the common position. that said, i am aware that parliament's assessment might be different and i fully respect it, as has been confirmed here today. the commission and council welcome in particular the proposals relating to some categories of products, such as products for children or intimate hygiene or the extension of the minimum durability date requirement. they would be of clear benefit to consumers. the commission shares parliament's concerns on the use of substances classified as carcinogenic, mutagenic or toxic for reproduction (cmr). the cosmetics directive already regulates the use of cmr substances in a sectoral manner. however, to find a consistent and sustainable solution for the substances specific provisions have been introduced to strengthen the legal framework, while complying with the key principle of risk assessment. furthermore, the commission has proposed new measures on these new substances in its white paper on the new chemicals policy and we are now working on concrete proposals with my colleague, mrs wallström. regarding the fragrance allergies issue, the commission and council welcome the introduction of a labelling system for allergenic fragrance ingredients. this will improve the current labelling requirements to allow consumers who are sensitive to certain ingredients to avoid products containing them. as regards the issue of animal testing, we all know how difficult and sensitive it is, especially because it creates a great deal of concern among the public. we all share the objective to reduce animal pain inflicted during experimentation. this issue is linked to many other policy areas and we are therefore working in constant and close cooperation with my colleagues, mr byrne and mr lamy, who are responsible for health and consumer protection and trade issues respectively. the council common position compromise has taken these aspects into account in a new global approach. it leads to a balanced solution offering a genuine benefit to animal welfare, whilst safeguarding consumer health and taking into account the impact of our internal decisions at international level. we thought that this approach would be a positive step and give a political signal that your concerns have been considered, as it reintroduced a marketing ban. the proposed solution to ban animal testing within the eu whenever possible and to make mandatory use of all alternative methods at the earliest opportunity seemed an improvement. it will lead to the management of animal testing within the european union, thereby answering the criticism of exporting the problem. acceptable validated alternative methods are still lacking for a number of key toxicological parameters. they are necessary for a full health risk assessment of cosmetic ingredients to ensure a high level of consumer protection. it is impossible to predict when all the necessary alternative methods might be available. finally, the european union is bound to ensure compliance with its international obligations, but an essential objective is to provide the highest possible level of safety. a total ban on all animal testing would endanger the safety of cosmetics and therefore human health. the marketing ban, linked to the acceptance at oecd level and excluding any cut-off date, reduces the risk of a challenge under the disputes settlement system of wto. the european union, with the strong support of the european parliament, is seeking to promote considerations relating to the welfare of animals with the wto. however, this proposal has so far found very little support among the wto members. some colleagues have argued that the eu should implement such a ban and see whether it is challenged by other wto members and then if necessary consider the measure in the light of wto opposition. i put the question to my colleagues who are responsible for wto relations and their reply is as follows: such a course of action has a political cost. we would reinforce the suspicion already widespread among developing countries that, regardless of their commitments in the wto agreements, developed countries intend to use their trade power to impose their own ethical priorities and values on others. to provoke a wto case under those circumstances might, therefore, be counterproductive, increasing the polarisation between developing and developed countries and reducing the chances of success of the eu's effort to bring such matters as animal welfare and environmental and social considerations into the wto discussions. that is the opinion of my colleagues who are responsible for this field in wto relations. i am convinced that further work, in close cooperation between parliament and the council and commission, will finally lead to a balanced solution that genuinely benefits animal welfare while safeguarding consumer health and our international commitments. mr jackson put two detailed questions on amendments nos 21 and 28. on the second part of amendment no 28, we could accept in principle the listing of these recognised fragrance allergens in annex 3, according to the suggested modalities, but it should be in the form of a commission directive. the commission would reject the first part. the proposed amendment to annex 3 has to be implemented via a commission directive adapting technical progress adopted under the comitology procedure. as far as amendment no 21 on fragrance listing is concerned, this amendment requires a full ingredient listing, including perfume composition. given the large number of ingredients in fragrance compositions, indicating all the fragrance ingredients on the labelling would neither be feasible nor helpful to consumers or dermatologists and would be disproportionate to the anticipated risks. furthermore this deletion would remove the legal basis for requesting that fragrances with potential allergenic effects be indicated as requested in amendment no 26, which has been introduced in the common position. in conclusion, the commission can accept in principle amendment no 1, first part, amendment no 5, first part, amendment no 27, second part and amendment no 28, second part. however, the commission cannot accept amendments nos 1, second part, amendments nos 2 to 5, second part, amendments nos 6 to 27, first part, amendment no 28, first part, and amendments nos 29 to 33.

- the agenda calls for the recommendation for second reading (a5-0141/2002), by mr nisticò, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, on the council's common position with a view to the adoption of quality and safety standards for the collection, testing, processing, storage and distribution of human blood and blood components (14402/1/2001 - c5-0069/2002 - 2000/0323 ((cod)).
- mr. nisticò, you asked to speak on a personal matter. on the basis of rule 122, i would ask you to wait until the end of the debate, because that rule provides that all personal interventions are heard at the end of the debate.
- mr president, commissioner, we are in the second reading of a very important directive concerning our strategy for the public health and safety of european citizens. the directive on the quality and safety of blood and blood products is receiving particular attention from the european parliament, because it is necessary to describe in strict scientific terms the conditions and conditions to be observed in order to ensure the greatest possible health of both donors and recipients of blood and blood products. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european union is able to take action to combat the spread of the disease, and that the european union is able to take action to combat it. i am glad that the european commission has adopted a number of important amendments at first reading, but it should also welcome a number of amendments which were not accepted at first reading but which have been tabled and voted on again in the committee on the environment. let me comment on some of them. i consider it an inalienable right of the patient who is about to receive a transfusion to be informed by those responsible of any side effects and the likelihood of them affecting his health. it goes without saying that the inviolability of personal data is guaranteed because no third party should have access to it. the commission's proposal is not acceptable. the person in charge of the center must be a medical doctor and even specialized in matters related to the subject of blood donation, since many times during the blood draw there are complications or side effects that require medical evaluation and treatment. i would also like to mention an ethical point. the european parliament has always been a party to the treaty of rome, and it has always been a party to the treaty of rome. i therefore agree with the principle of voluntary unpaid blood donation because i believe that, apart from the ethical issue, it makes a significant contribution to the quality and safety of blood. in conclusion, it would be a failure not to thank the rapporteur for his very substantial work.
commission. – mr president, it was in 1628 that doctor william harvey first postulated his theory on the true nature of blood circulation. it took about 200 years, however, for this theory to be fully accepted. thankfully, it has taken a significantly shorter period of time, less than a year and a half, for the council and parliament to consider the commission's proposal presented in january 2001 for a directive establishing quality and safety requirements for blood and blood components. the proposed amendments before you today are the culmination of considerable efforts on the part of the council in reaching its common position, the environment committee in proposing amendments to that position, and the rapporteur, in cooperation with the spanish presidency, in trying to reach a compromise. of the 28 amendments proposed by parliament, eight must be rejected by the commission. these are amendments nos 4, 7, 8, 11, 22, 23, 24 and 26. i will briefly give our reasoning on each of these. amendment no 4, refers to "products", a term that covers, in this context, any therapeutic product derived from blood or plasma, including medicinal products based on blood constituents and prepared industrially. the latter are covered by directive 2001/83, where technical requirements are specified in detail. as the term "products" in this amendment could lead to legal difficulties, it needs to be omitted. this also applies to amendments nos 5 and 6 and, to a certain extent, to amendment no 1, which is acceptable on balance but is somewhat superfluous without amendment no 4. with regard to amendment no 7, the commission accepts the view that the definition of "voluntary non-remunerated donation", as defined by the council of europe, should appear in the text of the directive. the discussions in council, however, have clearly demonstrated that this definition is not sufficiently precise to be included in the enacting terms of a legally binding directive. for these reasons, the commission prefers this definition to be incorporated in recital 23. we therefore reject amendment no 7 and accept the compromise proposal put forward by the shadow rapporteur in amendment no 19. amendments nos 8 and 27 propose two slightly different definitions of "haemovigilance". we accept amendment no 27 and reject amendment no 8 because amendment no 27 reflects more closely the directive's provisions on this issue. amendments nos 11 and 22 propose a preference for the responsible person to be a haematologist. as the tasks of the responsible person are managerial as well as technical, such a specialist may not always be required for this post. furthermore, it is not opportune to mention in a community legal text specific qualifications that are not harmonised at community level. member states, however, have the freedom to impose stricter requirements if they so wish. i recommend instead voting in favour of the compromise amendment no 28 by mr nisticò. amendment no 23 has to be rejected because it is not appropriate to modify the title of an article so that it no longer corresponds with its content. the issues presented in amendment no 24 have all been taken into account in the modifications to article 15 of the common position, together with a new definition of "haemovigilance" presented in amendment no 27, which the commission can accept. amendment no 26 has to be rejected. the establishment of any deadline for the introduction of binding requirements relating to voluntary unpaid donations cannot be considered at a time when member states are facing difficulties in securing an adequate supply of blood – a point made by many of the speakers here this morning. all other amendments are acceptable to the commission, either in full or in part. amendments nos 1, 9, 12, 15, 17, 19, 25, 27 and 28 are fully acceptable. allow me to elaborate briefly on those amendments that the commission is prepared to accept in part. these are: nos 2, 3, 5, 6, 10, 13, 14, 16, 18, 20 and 21. amendment no 2, as drafted, clearly is not in line with the limitations of article 152 of the treaty. the wording would need to be substantially revised to comply with the treaty. however, it would be a far better solution to drop the amendment entirely. with regard to amendments nos 3 and 21, in relation to personal genetic data, the commission fully supports the idea behind the proposal, but the examples given for potential misuse of data might be too specific. amendments nos 5 and 6 would only be acceptable if the word "products" were to be deleted for the reasons i have already given in my comments on amendment no 4. amendment no 10 would cause major problems. we believe that in article 4(2), on restrictions of imports among member states a reference to the treaty is clearly necessary in order to avoid any misinterpretation of this sensitive text. the wording in the compromise amendment no 20 by the shadow rapporteur provides this reference but the suggestion that voluntary unpaid donations alone can ensure a high level of health protection is scientifically doubtful. a revised wording indicating that voluntary unpaid donations can contribute to a high level of public health, as already stated in recital 23, would be acceptable. we are prepared, however, to accept the deletion of the specific reference to article 30 of the treaty. equally problematic is the proposed reference to article 19(1), which establishes a community objective but not a legal requirement. therefore it cannot, as such, be used to justify trade restrictions among member states, which are generally only acceptable for very substantial reasons. the danger here is that this provision might be vulnerable to legal challenge, which could lead to the directive being annulled. the commission fully agrees with the principle of regular reporting by member states on the issue of voluntary unpaid donations, as proposed in amendment no 13. however, this reporting requirement should be brought into line with the reports that member states have to submit every three years on the overall implementation of the directive. i note what mr nisticò said in his own contribution on this issue a moment ago. amendment no 14 can be accepted in a modified form. it would be necessary to make reference in article 20 to quality management, coupled with a new recital which could include all the extra detailed explanations required by the current amendment. the cross-reference to article 11 would provide safeguards for testing to be always carried out in accordance with good practice, while the new recital would provide an explanation for the principles behind the procedure. with regard to amendment no 16, the commission is ready to accept the first part, referring to genetic analyses, but not the second part because patents cannot be regulated under article 152. the idea behind amendment no 18 can be accepted in a slightly modified form within the first paragraph of article 23. to conclude, it is the commission's opinion that, with regard to the 28 amendments, agreement between all three institutions remains within our grasp. the european parliament is now at a critical juncture where it must make its decision on whether the proposed compromises are acceptable. this decision will either further the debate in the context of the conciliation procedure or enable the directive to go forward for final adoption at the forthcoming health council.
- mr nisticò has asked to speak on the basis of rule 122 of the rules of procedure, on a personal matter.
- this debate is over. the vote will take place at 12 noon today.
- the next item is the report (a5-0139/2002) by mrs klass, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, on the indication of ingredients in food (com (2001) 433 - c5-0404/2001 - 2001/0199 (cod)).
commission. – mr president, firstly i would like to thank the rapporteur, mrs klaß, and the members of the committee for all their hard work on this particular dossier. the question of food labelling is of great importance, as many people have said this morning. our legislation must satisfy the legitimate demands of consumers. after all, what could be more reasonable than wanting to know what foods are made up of, in order to make informed decisions in the face of an increasingly wide range of foods from which to choose. but even more important for a sadly ever-growing number of consumers, those who suffer from food allergies or intolerances, is information about the presence of certain ingredients for health reasons. it is therefore essential that labelling legislation not only ensures that all consumers receive comprehensive information, but also addresses the very real problem of adverse reactions to food. these are the objectives of the proposal we are discussing here today and i am pleased to note that in general it has attracted a broad consensus of support. in view of this, our discussions can focus mainly on the most appropriate means of achieving the objectives. labelling issues are often more complex than they at first appear. for example, some flavourings are spice mixtures containing dozens of ingredients. is it necessary, or even desirable, to list them all? i believe not, except in the case of substances derived from ingredients which can cause allergies or intolerances. to encapsulate the problem, just how precise does food labelling need to be? it is absolutely essential to avoid an excessive amount of information which would be difficult for consumers to take on board. quantity cannot be allowed to cloud quality and clarity. our proposal aims, therefore, to provide information that is sufficiently complete whilst guarding against the omission of any ingredients which might cause allergies or intolerances. at the same time, the obligation to provide detailed labelling must be accompanied by procedures enabling manufacturers to satisfy this obligation without excessive technical difficulties. it was with these considerations in mind that we examined the proposed amendments before us today. as the list is relatively short, i will comment on them all. amendment no 1 would abolish the possibility of having technical implementing rules adopted for the special case of alcoholic drinks under the sectoral management committee procedure. however, specific labelling provisions are in force for certain alcoholic drinks – wines, for example. this amendment would create inconsistency with existing legislation and therefore i cannot accept it. amendment no 2 would add mixtures of mushrooms to other mixtures, fruit and vegetables, which can be labelled without having to satisfy the rule of descending order of weight and specifies that this option would only apply in the case of mixtures whose proportions may vary. i accept that this point is valid and i therefore accept this amendment. amendment no 3 would abolish the possibility of flexibility in the labelling of ingredients used in small quantities – under 5% of the finished product. thus it would remove, firstly, the possibility of not strictly respecting the descending order of weight in the list of ingredients and, secondly, the possibility of using the expression "and/or" in the case of ingredients which are interchangeable according to availability. amendment no 13 would abolish only the first possibility. however, these provisions which allow for some flexibility of labelling presentation are technically justified, given the obligation to list all ingredients, including those used in very small quantities. i cannot, therefore, accept these amendments. amendment no 4 would abolish a requirement for an explanation as regards repetition where ingredients are used several times in the preparation of a product. this amendment provides for simplification and can be accepted. therefore amendment no 14, which would abolish the whole provision, cannot be accepted. amendment no 5 aims to remove the provision of not requiring the indication of the composition of compound ingredients used in small quantities – under 5% of the finished product – where the composition in question is covered by current community legislation which gives the composition corresponding to the trade name. this derogation would not, however, apply in either additives or allergens. the aim of this derogation is to avoid making a list of ingredients unnecessarily long, while remaining consistent with the objectives of the proposal. i cannot accept its removal and must therefore reject the amendment. amendment no 6 aims chiefly to abolish the derogation from labelling provided for in the proposal for ingredients which make up preparations of sauces and mustards constituting less than 5% of the foodstuffs. this derogation is also designed to avoid making the list of ingredients excessively long. the amendment would remove the advantage of keeping lists shorter. i cannot, therefore, accept amendment no 6. amendment no 7 comprises two distinct elements. first, it would exempt processing aids derived from allergenic ingredients from the requirement to be indicated on the label, on the grounds that these substances are eliminated during the manufacturing process. however, residues may be present in the finished product and may cause allergic reactions, even where mere residues or traces of allergens are present. this part of amendment no 7 is therefore not acceptable. second, it would make the european food safety authority responsible for establishing criteria for updating the annex and for revising it every two years. there is an ambiguity here, because amendment of the annex – which will be necessary – has to be done by the legislator, having consulted the authority on the scientific aspects. we can accept the principle of regular revision of the list annexed to the proposal but the amendment as it is currently worded is not acceptable. neither is amendment no 12 on the same issue. the purpose of amendments nos 8, 9 and 10 is to add ingredients to the list in the annex. however, the list proposed by the commission remains valid and should only be supplemented later on the basis of objective scientific criteria. with a view to subsequent updating of the list the commission is consulting the scientific committee for food and has made provisions for a rapid procedure to update the list. these amendments are therefore not acceptable. finally, i can agree on the principle that, as requested in amendment no 11, an interpretative guideline for annex iii(a) would have to be laid down, but only if this is necessary. my view is that this annex should be drawn up in such a way that no further interpretation guideline would be needed. i cannot, therefore, accept the current wording of this amendment. that concludes the summary of the commission view of the proposed amendments.
- this debate is over. the vote will take place at 12 noon today. (the sitting was suspended at 11.58 a.m. and resumed at 12 noon)

commission. - mr president, on 6 march 2002 the commission decided to initiate the normal investigation procedure under article 88 (2) of the ec treaty in respect of the possible misuse of state aid authorised by the commission in previous decisions of 1994 and 1998 and the legality of new aid recently granted to olympic airways. the greek government was notified of the decision and submitted its comments on the matter on several occasions. the commission decision was also published in the official journal, so that interested third parties could submit their comments to the commission within one month. that period expired on 23 may 2002, the date on which the commission received comments from interested parties. the commission will take a final decision after having carefully examined the comments submitted by the greek authorities and the interested third parties appearing on the file. the commission is not in a position to comment on the merits of the case before it has delivered its final opinion on the allegations raised in its decision of 6 march 2002.

rapporteur. – mr president, the specific programme for research and training on nuclear energy provides eur 940 million for activities in three priority areas – controlled thermonuclear fusion, management of radioactive waste and radiation protection – as well as other activities in the field of nuclear technology and safety. the bulk of the funds – eur 750 million – is proposed to be used for nuclear fusion research, with a maximum of eur 200 million provided for iter, the next step tokamak device. subject to a positive outcome of the international negotiations, a specific decision is expected in the period 2003-2004, so that construction of iter could effectively start during the period 2005-2006. this will be a major step towards sustained production of energy from nuclear fusion, which is expected to become an efficient source of energy within a few decades. in view of the limited deposits of fossil fuel, the importance of this development cannot be underestimated. consequently, the associated research fully deserves the proposed funding. given the fact that the final decision on iter has not been taken yet, the flexibility in partitioning funds between iter and other fusion activities is an important element of the proposal. other activities within the field of controlled nuclear fusion include the associations' programme in physics and technology and the exploitation of jet facilities. the associations' programme includes, among others, studies of magnetic confinement schemes other than the tokamak, in particular the continuation of the construction of the wendelstein 7-x "stellarator", research on fusion materials and keeping in touch with civil research activities on inertial confinement and possible alternative concepts. the joint european taurus (jet) in culham, england, has been a highly successful machine and its facilities will continue to be exploited, but will have to be phased out at an appropriate time to enable the corresponding resources to be redirected to iter. regardless of the future of nuclear fusion reactors, the problem of radioactive waste – in particular long-lived waste – will be with us for several thousands of years. consequently, it is only by an increase of the research efforts that we may hope to decrease the dangers emanating from them. the specific programme provides for eur 90 million to be used for research on geological disposal and the development of concepts to produce less waste. radiation protection takes up to eur 50 million. finally, the chapter of "other activities" covers the evaluation of innovative concepts and the development of improved and safer nuclear energy processes, education and training in radiation protection, improvement of the safety of existing installations, for a total of eur 50 million. the committee on industry, external trade, research and energy welcomes the commission's proposal and expresses its support for fusion research in general and iter in particular. a european site for iter will confirm the worldwide leadership of the european union in fusion technology, acquired through, among other things, the jet facilities, which should not be abandoned before exhausting its capabilities. a number of amendments proposed by our committee refer to nuclear waste and nuclear safety. ways of dealing with waste should be not only acceptable to society, but also intrinsically safe. at this point, i should like to point out that there is a mistake in the french translation of amendment no 4, where the original english text "both safe and acceptable" has been transmuted into "both healthy and acceptable". could the president ensure that this is corrected? the research on interim disposal, waste characterisation and waste packaging, should be funded, together with research on geological disposal. research on innovative concepts and techniques that produce less waste should include the high-temperature reactor, light-water reactors, gas-cooled reactors, fast reactor technologies and co-generation. overall, the adoption of the report by the committee on industry will mark an important step towards more efficient and safer nuclear energy.

- madam president, commissioner, mr president-in-office of the council, ladies and gentlemen, on 18 april 2002 the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities adopted an opinion, drawn up by mrs dybkjær, on the basis of the commission's reports submitted in november 2001. the other very important report by mr brok unfortunately did not include any of our proposals on the legal and social position of women in the candidate countries and, as chairman of the committee on women's rights, i would like to express to the house the strong dissatisfaction of my committee. i hope, of course, that the amendments tabled by my committee on this subject will be adopted tomorrow. in its opinion, our committee stressed that the adoption of the community acquis in the field of equality between men and women is a sine qua non for accession, since it is fundamentally a human rights issue. institution building, strengthening institutional and administrative capacity in this area is a key prerequisite for the full implementation of the european acquis. gender equality is a fundamental and integral part of economic, social and democratic development and should therefore be a guiding principle of the development and integration process. the candidate countries should therefore ensure the full and effective implementation of the acquis in relation to equal opportunities for men and women before accession. as is well known, in the field of equal opportunities, the community acquis contains nine directives on equal pay, equal treatment at work, safety and health, work for pregnant women, parental leave, the burden of proof in cases of discrimination and equal treatment in matters of social policy. we also believe that it is very important for the applicant countries to develop or renew their judicial and administrative structures in order to create the necessary conditions for the effective application of the acquis communautaire. the committee on women's rights has of course taken account of any progress made in the candidate countries and has pointed out areas where further improvement is needed. we believe that effective measures to prevent and combat trafficking in women, prostitution, the sexual exploitation of minors and child pornography should be an important part of an integrated enlargement process in the accession countries, since many of these countries are still the countries of origin, transit and destination of the international organized crime networks involved in the modern slave trade. in addition, some of the candidate countries are in a difficult situation with regard to children, a situation which is a direct consequence of the weakness of social protection systems. the commission is called upon to intensify its efforts to eliminate all forms of violence against women as part of the accession strategy to ensure respect for women's individual rights and dignity, and certainly to provide incentives for improving children's rights and child protection. we also need a broad programme of institutional interventions and financial support to integrate the gender dimension into the economic guidelines of the accession process, in order to offset the negative effects on gender equality of the process of privatisation and cuts in public social spending. as is well known, the transition to a market economy is putting great pressure on employment in many of the candidate countries. this raises particular concern about the negative impact of this transition process on women, as they are the first victims of budget cuts in childcare, education and social protection systems supporting work-life balance. the commission needs to promote appropriate public and private investment in social sectors in the candidate countries in order to facilitate and strengthen the participation of women in representative institutions and decision-making centres. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. in this context, we ask the commission to inform us of what has been achieved in the candidate countries in the field of the adoption of the community acquis on equal opportunities. we would also like to know to what extent the candidate countries have advanced the building of the relevant institutional framework during the same period... - mr president, the soccer world cup is certainly an international sporting event, which attracts the interest of millions of people all over the world. in the shadow of this event, thousands of children devote their precious time daily to making balls used in soccer games. these children are denied every opportunity to get a better education in school or to play like other children their age. child labour and inhumane working conditions for adults who manufacture footballs are unfortunately still common practice, despite the agreements concluded by the international football federation with the sports equipment companies. according to the report by the international network against child labour, aptly titled "the dark side of football", pakistan is the country that produces the most footballs in the world, followed by india. in one region of india alone, punjab, some 10 000 children aged 6 to 12 are working in the sewing of footballs, while some 15 000 children are working in the talkot region of pakistan. these balls, made by children's hands for a few cents, are the same balls that are sold in western sporting goods stores with the logos of well-known companies, even fifa, for tens of dollars. despite efforts to address this sad phenomenon with programs and control systems, both by international organizations and by the sports equipment companies themselves, it is found that manufacturing has been moved to small settlements where either policing is lacking or children are no longer working in factories but at home. also, according to the times of london, many children were found making soccer balls bearing the names of well-known multinational companies. we call for this world cup, dedicated to children, to be an opportunity for international organisations and sports equipment manufacturers, as well as governments, to work together to adopt a reliable system of monitoring compliance with labour rules. because child labour is neither a hobby nor a game, i expect both fifa and the industries to take the lead in making football a truly fair game for all. we have to get a message across today that adults should be given work while children are in school. adults at work, kids at school.

the rapporteur. - mr president, we are discussing today the legislative proposal for a regulation concerning, firstly, the traceability and labelling of genetically modified organisms, secondly, the traceability of food and feed produced from genetically modified organisms and, thirdly, the amendment of directive 2001/18 concerning the deliberate release into the environment of genetically modified organisms. the main aim is to ensure harmonisation of rules within the european union with a view to the effective functioning of the internal market and better consumer information. the commission's proposal, and the amendments adopted by the committee on the environment, do not, unfortunately, provide any guarantee that these objectives will be achieved. on the contrary, they open the way to fraud and deception, to misleading the consumer, to distortion of prices and competition and to less favourable treatment of producers and businesses within the european union. this proposal relates to the cartagena protocol on biosafety, but this protocol does not contain any provision on the traceability of products derived from genetically modified organisms. this does not provide legal certainty and is a weakness, perhaps even an obstacle, to the process of establishing rules within the community, not only for domestic community products but also for imported ones. this is because there is no legal obligation to provide all the data at every stage of the production process. this opens the way to all kinds of fraud and deception on the part of producers and suppliers from third countries. the result will be a distortion of competition because, while producers in the european union will be obliged to follow the regulation, producers and suppliers from third countries will be able to claim that products are made from natural materials, but no one will be able to check and establish whether this is actually the case. moreover, the unilateral application by the european union of traceability and labelling requirements on imported products and control measures without sufficient scientific evidence could create a trade dispute with third countries and refer the matter to the organs of the world trade organisation. the proposal also fails to promote a technology of the future, such as biotechnology, which is universally recognised and holds great promise for human society in the 21st century. i am therefore surprised and i wonder who is making such proposals. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on genetically modified organisms are applied in the community. in addition, the methods we have today are extremely sensitive to the detection of genetically modified dna or genetically modified protein. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the first category of products - those containing modified dna or protein; so we can use traceability and tagging on these. the second category, however, is products derived from genetically modified organisms but containing neither dna nor protein nor any other characteristic different from conventional products. but when we treat products that are exactly the same in terms of labelling, we treat them unequally, and they have different purchasing power and of course they distort prices and of course they mislead the buyer, who will eventually pay more. this type of treatment therefore distorts the market, confuses the consumer and in fact opens the way to fraud, because the labelling cannot be checked and the truth of what is stated on the label cannot be scientifically verified. i think it is therefore clear that the commission's proposal is so contradictory, and indeed as it was formulated in the committee on the environment, that if we accept its principles, we will be led to abolish any scientific basis and logic and proceed to food labelling based on political convictions or even ideological views. for animals that eat, for example, gmo products for 10 days, for 20 days, we will label meat, milk, butter, cheese and that is the logical extension of the commission's proposal. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the proposal is totally unworkable. it does not provide legal certainty because it cannot be accepted in any jurisdiction, such as the courts. my proposal to link traceability and labelling with the principle of identification and detection of genetically modified dna and protein is therefore the only one that is practically applicable to products of genetically modified organisms, ensuring the application of this regulation through scientific control.
- mr president, today we are not discussing whether genetically modified food is good or bad or dangerous to our health, although there are certainly well-founded suspicions about it. today we are discussing the question of ensuring the right of citizens to know and choose among the products offered to them. to this end, products must be identified and bear a mark indicating that identity. it is now technically possible to make this identity correspond to the real history of the product. what materials it is made of and how it is made. i therefore do not agree with any amendment which seeks to restrict the detection of genetically modified organisms to the final form of the product, whereas we can, in accordance with the commission's proposals, have a system which gives us more information at all stages of production, and thus a more precise, more complex picture of the origin of the final product. the simple application of the precautionary principle and the concern for the freedom of information and choice of the citizen are reasons for supporting the commission's basic proposals and for strengthening their guidelines, rather than taking a more conservative position in parliament, which can also be accused of concealing information. i include in this support the need to detect genetically modified organisms - and in animal feed, of course - and i would add, mr president, that for accidental contamination the 1% threshold seems to me to be too high. i hear and hear that 0.1% is possible and i do not understand then why we do not institutionalize it. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. it has introduced a system of centralised control, mainly through the food safety authority. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full, and i am sure that the commission will take the necessary steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full.
- mr president, the communist party of greece is in radical disagreement with the directive which frees up genetically modified organisms because we believe that they do not protect public health and the environment, but the profits of a few multinationals who, among other things, will control food production. in the name of scientific progress, which is exploited by large food multinationals, human intervention in the genetic codes of living organisms is being attempted, while no one can guarantee the effects of mutated species which are neither predictable nor immediately apparent and will only be perceived by the disasters they will create. although we consider the european parliament's amendments to be more positive, there is a serious possibility that they will have more negative effects, because the responsibility for suitability is transferred with the labels from the competent public bodies to the consumers, who do not always have the capacity and the appropriate knowledge to evaluate them. moreover, even if these proposals were to have any effect, the time-span of benefit would be limited, since with the contamination process and the commission's proposal to consider products containing 1% genetically modified organisms as clean, in a very few years all products will be contaminated, and in particular plants, through cross-pollination. we are very much afraid that what is being done is focused on reassuring public opinion, on making the risks look good and on creating a climate of acceptance of gmos, favouring multinational food companies and the multiplication of their profits, leaving dangerous scope for undermining the health of consumers and the balance of the ecosystem.

commission. – in the context of meetings between the commission and national authorities in order to examine the application of european community law in member states – the so-called package meetings – a delegation from the commission environment directorate-general visited greece in may this year. the main objective of the meeting was to debate on issues related to atmospheric pollution. in particular the commission inquired about measures adopted by the greek authorities to transpose and implement directive 96/61, the so-called ippc, and reviewed technical issues related to infringements and complaints concerning bad application of directive 84/360, concerning combating air pollution from industrial plants. in addition, the greek authorities provided updated information on several other infringements, including the caretta caretta case – the loggerhead sea turtle. the greek authorities have promised to submit to the commission detailed replied for all those cases. the information contained in those replies will be assessed with a view to checking whether greece has respected its obligations under european community environmental legislation. in particular regarding the measures to comply with the court's judgment concerning failure to establish and implement an effective system of strict protection of the caretta caretta sea turtle, the greek authorities have described the system of protection which contains the legislative and concrete protection measures. the written notification to the commission is under way.

the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i have to say that i am very pleased with the way the commission has been working. first of all, i want to thank you very much for this invitation to come to andalusia. i love this part of spain, as you know. we must of course organise this in a proper way, but i am in principle prepared to go to andalusia, as i do to many other parts of the community's coastal regions. i understand your concern about small-scale fishing and it is true that these smaller vessels tend to have a competitive disadvantage compared to the larger vessels. when we in the commission took decisions on the proposals for fisheries reform, we also said that we were prepared to discuss with the member states in the council negotiations, and of course with parliament, ways of helping this small-scale coastal fishery in particular. we are in complete agreement here. i too believe that we must do everything we can to preserve this small coastal fishery.

the rapporteur. - madam president, we are discussing today the joint draft decision establishing a programme of community action in the field of public health for the period 2003-08, which was unanimously approved on 8 may by parliament's delegation to the conciliation committee with the council. the joint project is the culmination of a long-term effort which began two years ago and which, i must say, has been very constructive both in terms of the cooperation of the three institutions and in the final formulation of the programme itself. the establishment of the community action programme in the field of public health constitutes the first integrated project of the european union in the field of public health protection. the protection of public health is a major concern for the citizens of the union today. health must be a priority over political or economic compromises. the programme, which will replace the sectoral and piecemeal community health actions from 1 january 2003 with a more integrated programme, is mainly geared to disease prevention and will contribute to the establishment of quality standards in the field of public health. taking into account the experience gained under the eight previous programmes and the work of the community epidemiological surveillance and control network for communicable diseases, the programme seeks to pursue a more integrated and comprehensive strategy in health. the three general objectives are: first, to improve information and knowledge for the development of public health; the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to cover the following areas: firstly, the protection of the environment and the environment, secondly, a rapid and coordinated response to health threats and, thirdly, the promotion of health and the prevention of disease by addressing the determinants of health in all policy activities. these lifestyle factors, such as smoking, alcohol, diet, and major socioeconomic and environmental factors, together with genetic factors, constitute the main causes of disease. the programme, which will complement national policies, aims to achieve a high level of physical and mental health and well-being and greater health equity throughout the community. i would like to make a brief assessment of the work of the conciliation committee, which is a major improvement on the text and the council's common position. the budget of eur 312 million is considerably higher than the amount of eur 280 million proposed by the council and even higher than the amount of eur 300 million proposed by the european commission. the increase is in line with the ambitious objectives of the programme and the requirements of the addition of certain additional actions. this is a remarkable achievement, because it is the second time, according to parliament's data, since the start of the co-decision procedure that the amount finally approved is higher than that proposed by the european commission. in addition, the commission's statement on article 7 on financing provides that the financial framework will be reviewed both in the context of the accession of the new member states and in preparation for the revision of the financial perspective. to ensure the effective implementation and coordination of the programme, a second statement by the european commission was made on structural arrangements to enable the programme to function better. finally, most of the european parliament's amendments, 37 of the 50 proposed at second reading, were accepted by the council, either in their entirety or in their form, and are included in the joint text. they're about addressing health threats from physical, chemical, biological sources, emergencies, vaccination and immunization strategies, mental health, antibiotic resistance, real public health problems very, very important. i believe that strengthening the foundations of the european construction requires convergence, not only in the economic policy field but also in individual sectors such as health. in full respect of the principle of subsidiarity, it is possible to move towards new convergences that extend european integration and i therefore consider that the new programme is the beginning of such a process in the vital field of health. in conclusion, i would like to thank all the members of the delegation who worked for the successful outcome of the conciliation, the commission and commissioner byrne personally, and the spanish presidency for the constructive cooperation with the parliament's delegation in reaching an agreement on the basis of which the programme can be rapidly implemented. i therefore recommend that the european parliament approve the text of the programme and thus launch this important and pioneering programme.
- mr president, commissioner, today we are in the final stages of adopting an ambitious programme for public health. the programme has three objectives: to improve information and knowledge, to respond rapidly to health threats and to address the determinants of health through health promotion and disease prevention. the creation of a mechanism to ensure continuous monitoring and coordination, to enhance efficiency and to provide information to member states on best practices and overall evaluation of the programme is essential for the implementation of the above. i believe that a compromise has been reached here which guarantees the correct implementation of the programme in the direction in which parliament's amendments were intended. the commission has also taken the initiative of proposing a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens and to the risks related to exposure to carcinogens. the compromise reached with the council on this point is satisfactory. i would also like to mention the budget for the programme. i believe that if we are to meet the requirements and objectives of this programme, we must secure the necessary resources. the european parliament had proposed the amount of eur 380 million and i think that this should have been accepted by the council, since a large part of this money has been absorbed by the programmes already in progress by the end of 2002. the new programme will also cover not only the 15 member states but also the 10 new members. finally, action on bioterrorism was added to the initial objectives after the events of 11 september. the final amount of eur 312 million approved, although higher than that which came from the commission and council proposals, does not satisfy me. i am glad, however, that the commissioner responsible, mr byrne, has committed himself to reviewing the financial framework after the entry of the new member states. this was a decisive factor in the final vote during the conciliation. i would like to thank the rapporteur for his full cooperation and for the efforts he has made to reach a proper compromise.
- mr president, mrs van lancker's report deals with a subject which has been of particular concern to the committee on women's rights and my political group, but also to each of us individually. it has given us the opportunity to see two things. on the one hand, there is a lack of information in modern society on issues related to sexual and reproductive health, such as communicable diseases, methods of treating them, methods of contraception, their advantages and disadvantages. we also noted the differences and gaps in some countries in family planning policies, sexual education, sexual and reproductive health services and agreed to share effective practices and promote modern policies. we have had a modern dialogue between the political groups in the committee and with representatives of european society, and we have agreed on the importance of all these issues for the modern life of european citizens, and especially women. but we have also noticed something else, and i would say that we have received particularly strong reactions from governments, political parties and representatives of women's organisations, because we are making reservations and giving directives - not to mention decisions - on matters which are not within the european competence. this concern has been a political message of particular importance to my group at a time when we are trying to define our responsibilities at european and national level, to convince the peoples of the european union and the candidate countries that we are working with respect for democracy, subsidiarity and cultural specificities, and we have received the message that we are not entitled to ask the member states to legalise abortion or not to prosecute women who have had an illegal abortion or to administer the morning-after pill without a prescription. we'll confuse and arouse the public's suspicion. when i visited poland with my colleagues from the committee on women's rights, we all stressed the importance of a dialogue on women's rights at all levels and in all spheres of life, and of policies evolving and adapting to social and medical conditions, but europe will not come to the level of ideology, morality and conscience, nor of cultural differences. i acknowledge the efforts of the rapporteur and her good will to agree a text and i personally hoped that we could reach a text that was acceptable to us and to the peoples of the european union. unfortunately, the message of the report is not what we as the european people's party believe the european parliament should be sending, and for that reason we will not vote in favour of the report.
- mr president, i think that mrs van lancker deserves our warmest congratulations and our highest praise for having taken the initiative of this important report which has opened up the debate and highlighted a problem which touches the very heart of the rights and fundamental freedoms of half the world's population, women. despite improvements in women's sexual and reproductive health in recent years, indicators still show high rates of abortion, including among adolescents, low use of contraceptive methods and an increase in sexually transmitted diseases. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment from harmful substances. the situation is very characteristic of the mobilisation of conservative and religious circles, which have in recent days flooded us with various publications in order to prevent the vote on this important report. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rights of women are protected, and that the right to vote is guaranteed. these are the well-known champions of gender equality and women's rights, and we also know that when they talk about morality, they usually mean the double morality of different measures and weights for both sexes. of course, abortion is not advocated by anyone as a means of contraception, but it is often a necessity and is usually done under the pressure of adverse social, economic, psychological and cultural conditions. does anyone doubt that many pregnancies are the result of violence of any kind against women? but beyond women's health and rights, should we not ensure that children are born into environments where they are welcome, so as to ensure their sound mental, physical and spiritual development? it is worth noting that the data show that the number of abortions is very low where high quality and free reproductive health and contraceptive services are provided, in countries where material support is given to pregnant women in difficulty, such as single women, where liberal legislation on termination of pregnancy is combined with serious and effective sex education and information, especially for young people. in conclusion, i believe that the european union and the council, as well as the acceding countries, should intensify their efforts to give priority to and have an integrated european policy on reproductive health, with emphasis on prevention, contraception and sexual education and information for young people. the principle of subsidiarity must not be used as a pretext for preventing the european parliament from taking a position on such an important matter.
mr president, i would also like to thank the danish prime minister, but there was a position on the cyprus issue that was a surprise for me. i think there is a small, delicate and, i hope, unintentional change in the text of the resolution of the helsinki european council. the resolution says that the decision on the accession of cyprus will be taken without a political solution being a pre-condition. here, the danish prime minister has said that "a final decision will be taken on the basis of all relevant factors". this is something very different from mr prodi's position, as well as the commission's position, mr verheugen's position, from the european parliament's position, from the helsinki position and from the position of the spanish presidency. i hope that there is no different intention on the part of the danish presidency and i am expecting to hear confirmation in the second speech by the danish prime minister, that, although we want a political solution and will try for one, it will not be a pre-condition for the accession of cyprus.
in writing. - (el) we are categorically opposed to the ruthless and purely monetary policy of the ecb, a policy of inequality, of increased exploitation of workers and of safeguarding the profits and privileges of european monopolies. in the name of strict compliance with the stability pact and the fight against inflation, there are attempts to reduce wages constantly and to impose structural changes on the labour market, with the main aim of achieving even greater flexibility in working conditions. in the name of what is called a 'healthy environment' for business and investment, the greed of big business is being reinforced, with no regard for the economic and social repercussions, the uncertainty and insecurity faced by workers and the heavy financial burdens they bear. the ecb's policy of raising or lowering its interest rates in line with the us interest rate policy is in fact dictated by the need to finance lucrative projects, which, from an objective point of view, lead not only to unproductive investments but also to 'combinations', particularly on the stock markets, which are financed by thousands of dismissed workers and investors. the scandals which have recently hit the american stock markets, first with enron, then with worldcom and xerox, and now also affecting european companies, have resulted in the looting of the savings of small investors, the stripping of the reserves of pension funds and insurance companies and the sacrifice of thousands of workers on the altar of superprofits.
- mr president, mr medina and mr marinho have systematically tried to highlight the shortcomings of the present community legislation on timeshare and to make it clear that for timeshare to be effective, the rules must not be one-sided. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the new legislative proposal which will follow the recommendation should, firstly, provide comprehensive answers to the problems faced by consumers up to now, secondly, prevent practices that circumvent the guarantees laid down in community legislation and, thirdly, provide safeguards for all parties involved. it is therefore necessary to include all new forms of contract for the rotational use of immovable property within the scope of the directive, without reference to fixed time limits, both as regards the minimum duration of the contract and as regards the annual period of use. rules are also needed: firstly, to guarantee that agents will meet their contractual obligations even in the event of bankruptcy and insolvency for a long period of time and that restrictions will be placed on the issuing of agency licences; secondly, on the consumer's right and the undertaking's obligation to buy his ticket at a fixed price; and thirdly, on the system of ticket exchange. the commission has also been active in the field of the protection of the environment, and has been involved in the development of the european environment agency. it is therefore appropriate to prohibit jurisdiction clauses referring to offshore jurisdictions or simply to jurisdictions other than the place where the property is situated, as well as clauses referring to the law of a third country. as mentioned above, in order to avoid unilateral regulation, even the interests of the property owners must be addressed. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the institution. finally, in the light of experience to date, the possibility of launching an information campaign at european level to promote trustworthy operators should be considered.
