the council. - mr president, the council is fully aware of the situation of human rights and fundamental freedoms in cuba and of the need to establish a system based on freedoms and other rights. such a system is provided for in the cuban constitution but has not been implemented. this is the main reason why cuba is today the only country in latin america with which the eu has not concluded a cooperation agreement. since 1996, the eu's relations with cuba have been defined by the eu's common position on cuba. as stated in the common position, the objective of relations with cuba is to promote the transition to a pluralist democracy and respect for human rights and freedoms, as well as a sustainable economic recovery and an improvement in the living standards of the cuban people. we believe that results-oriented dialogue and humanitarian aid are the best means of achieving this objective. the dialogue with cuba, which had been interrupted by the cuban authorities in april 2000, was officially resumed on 1 and 2 december last year when the eu troika of senior officials travelled to havana. the troika met the cuban delegation headed by foreign minister felipe perez roque. the eu troika also met with the cuban delegation at expert level when the un commission on human rights met in april this year. the general affairs council, meeting in luxembourg on 17 june this year, approved the 11th assessment of the eu's common position on cuba. the council reaffirmed the continuing relevance of these objectives. the council considered it important to continue the dialogue with cuba in order to achieve concrete results and also indicated that it expected clear signals from the cuban government towards the achievement of the objectives of the common position. on 20 may this year, when the varela project was presented to the cuban national assembly, the presidency made a statement on behalf of the european union welcoming the varela initiative. we believe that the varela project is an important initiative which, with the strong support of cuban civil society, can pave the way for the changes that the country needs and that cuban society demands. the initiative, which is based on the cuban constitution, aims to establish a system of freedoms and other rights which are enshrined in the constitution itself but which have not been implemented. in the statement, the eu expresses its hope that the national assembly will consider the initiative and that the varela project will be the starting point for a debate that can promote a peaceful transition to a pluralist democracy and a cuban society at peace with itself. at its meeting in luxembourg on 17 june, the council took note of this 11th assessment of the eu's common position on cuba. it was confirmed that the common position continues to form the basis of the eu's policy towards cuba and it was noted that this policy is still relevant. the council noted some positive signs, such as greater religious freedom, fewer political prisoners, no use of the death penalty for the last two and a half years and the ratification of several un human rights instruments. it noted that there had been recent signs of a more open attitude on the part of the cuban authorities. this was considered a start and positive steps were expected from the cuban government to lead to concrete reforms towards a political system based on democratic values. in this context, the council is following with interest the development of the project in question, which is enshrined in the constitution, and invites the cuban government to regard it as a legitimate initiative, since it represents a significant effort to introduce these reforms. the conclusion is therefore that the council is very much concerned with cuba, is following developments in cuba, is making demands on cuba and is continuing to make demands on cuba, but at the same time is noting the modest progress that is being made, after all, and which the council hopes can lead to more in the future.

the council. - mr president, the council attaches great importance to the applicant countries' fulfilling the political criteria for accession set out in copenhagen in 1993. that means democracy, the rule of law, human rights and the protection of minorities. it was decided that these criteria had to be met before accession negotiations could be opened. the negotiations with slovakia were therefore only opened when the european council in helsinki in december 1999 was able to conclude that the criteria were now met. the commission concluded in its 2001 progress report that slovakia continued to meet the copenhagen political criteria. the commission also noted that since 1999 the country had done much to further consolidate and entrench the stability of its institutions, thereby strengthening democracy, the rule of law and human rights. as regards the specific question raised by the honourable member, the bill in question is currently, as far as is known, being considered by the slovak parliament. the second reading, which was due to take place in mid-june, may be postponed because of the large number of legislative proposals to be considered. the council cannot at this stage assess the content of this proposal. it would be inappropriate for the eu to interfere in the democratic process of a third country. if the draft law is adopted, the eu will undoubtedly take a position on its content and should it prove to be a violation of the fundamental democratic principles, in particular the copenhagen criteria, the eu will of course not hesitate to raise this issue at any given opportunity, and in particular in the bodies set up under the europe agreement. the council is therefore closely following developments with regard to this law and awaits further details of the debate in the slovak legislature.

in writing. - (el) the plenary assembly adopted today a resolution to protest against the recent us law which excludes us personnel from the jurisdiction of the newly established international criminal court, even when they are accused of committing genocide or war crimes. there is no doubt that the scope of this law violates the fundamental principles of international law and demonstrates the presumptuous, bellicose, hegemonic and imperialist nature of the united states. but any honest person would ask himself a few questions: 1.is it now that the eu has remembered the principles of international criminal law? why did it not apply them when, together with the united states, it bombed yugoslavia and, after 9/11, declared 'the war on terrorism' and 'war on afghanistan', proclaiming 'we are all americans'? 2. the on the basis of what principles did it establish, together with the united states, the international criminal court, an illegal body under the un charter, to try president milosevic and those who opposed the intervention of the united states and nato? 3. the will the leaders of the eu and nato be brought before this court for the crimes they committed in yugoslavia, afghanistan and elsewhere in the past? 4. the could it be that this court is finally serving to judge the opponents of their new imperialist order? in view of these considerations, the members of the communist party of greece cannot vote in favour of the resolution.
in writing. - (el) the new common fisheries policy is still based on the reduction of the fishing capacity of the member states, which is reflected in the reduction of the fishing fleet and, in general, of the fisheries themselves. in other words, it follows on from the previous cfp, which already had a serious impact on small and medium-sized fisheries undertakings. the proposal for a revision will multiply these effects, since the destruction of fishing vessels will be intensified and the possibilities for modernising other vessels will be reduced, if they do not stop, especially in small and medium-sized enterprises. all these measures, which are supposed to protect fish stocks and sustainable development, are in fact adopted to restrict access to fish resources by small fishermen and to hand them over to big business and its fishing enterprises. to make it easier and to avoid any reactions, this attempt is supposedly accompanied by measures to provide early retirement and retraining for fishermen who will turn to other professions. the prospect and consequences of the new cfp will be tragic for the eu regions whose economies are historically based on fishing. the implementation of the new measures will accelerate the rate of retirement of fishermen, cause them to lose a significant part of their income, inflate unemployment figures and intensify the trend towards abandonment of remote and island regions.

last year. belarus refuses to cooperate with the osce, which it has accused of meddling in its internal affairs. we strongly believe that the osce should continue to maintain a presence in belarus and commission. – the commission regrets the acts of violence which occurred in the city of buenos aires. the commission agrees that the deep root of these acts lies in a monetary crisis, which transformed itself into a commercial and economic crisis (sharp fall in trade, investment and gdp), deteriorated into a social crisis (unemployment, poverty and social exclusion) and is ending in a crisis of confidence in the national institutions. it is very complicated to analyse the reasons for the crisis in a resolution or discussion in parliament, but on the other hand it is very easy to blame the international organisations. the measures linked to the so-called "coralito", the peso/dollar parity introduced by law, and the resulting lack of any room for manoeuvre on monetary policy, among other examples, do not correspond to the imf recommendations. now there is a need for reforms. the reforms that argentina has to carry out are necessary not only to obtain help from the imf, but more importantly to get the country moving again. what is needed is to win back citizens’ confidence in the democratic institutions, to win back the confidence of economic actors in the stability and credibility of law and order, and, in particular, to restore the credibility of the financial system. the european union and its member states have already tried to help argentina in the different international organisations, including the imf and the last g8 summit. the commission, in particular, has a very firm idea of what to do. something that would undoubtedly help argentina is deeper integration of the mercosur countries and the european union is making an effort to accelerate its negotiations with mercosur. to this end, commissioners lamy and patten will hold a meeting with the mercosur ministers on 23 july in brasilia. the negotiations between mercosur and the european union will undoubtedly help promote internal cohesion in mercosur that will in turn help argentina. in terms of traditional cooperation, the financial resources that we have at our disposal are disproportionately low compared to the problem we are debating. however, our country strategy paper has identified the fight against poverty and institutional support as the two main priorities.

mr president, ladies and gentlemen, while naturally agreeing with the main idea, i am of the opinion that in the matter of the single european sky, which is a very complex matter, local specificities must be taken seriously into account and the advocates of the privatisation of air traffic management and control must find convincing arguments. it must also be designed, in cooperation with the military sector, in such a way as not to affect its autonomous decisions on defence matters and not to go beyond the current level of common foreign and defence policy. it is clear that some issues need further clarification. the commission has also proposed that the member states should be able to provide for the establishment of a european air traffic control system, which would be compatible with the common market. let's get this investigated. furthermore, despite the initial opinion of the council's legal service, the airspace above international waters does not appear to be covered. for a country like mine, for example, and others, the issue is very serious, since the lack of continuity of airspace obviously creates both technical and institutional problems, with the possibility of different institutional regimes within the same member state, with the consequent problems in everyday practice, and thus obviously the sought-after flexibility is being negated. the military sector's involvement in the whole project is still not clearly enshrined in national security matters, i.e. second pillar issues. the commission's proposal for a regulation on the harmonization of the conditions for the award of public supply contracts is a very good one. the nature of these does not appear to justify the application of article 80 (2) of the treaty alone, since they are not only a matter of transport. in conclusion, i believe that in order to satisfy the most serious of these concerns, the right to defence and security, as defined by national legislation, should be clearly guaranteed in the institution of the single european sky we want. i thank you and i congratulate the commissioner and her worthy colleagues on their very good work.

in writing. - (el) the report on trade and development is part of a wider debate within the eu on what is known as globalisation and its impact on third world countries. this debate shows that the major imperialist powers are concerned about the different reactions of the people to the impact of the global domination of big business and the fact that international trade must meet the demands of big business. this debate would have us believe that "globalization" can work miracles and solve the problems of development and poverty, once one or the other problem is solved. the aim is to encourage multinationals to develop a sense of responsibility, to set up investment ethics committees and to apply a 'fair' (but indefinite) trading system and a comprehensive trade liberalisation policy. the whole debate deliberately ignores the real causes and mechanisms used to plunder the wealth of poor countries. it ignores the fact that it is impossible to see multinational companies doing business and people prospering at the same time. it ignores the direct or indirect use of violence to force poor countries to accept trade terms that are profitable to big business and is therefore hypocritical and deceptive. for these reasons, the members of the delegation of the greek communist party to the european parliament voted against the deva report, which is little more than a manifesto of the 'globalization' party.
the commission is not prepared to accept this. since the start of direct talks on 16 january 2002, the leaders of the two communities have put forward proposals on the four key issues. however, the parties were unable to reach a comprehensive solution in line with the relevant un resolutions. as the direct talks are confidential in nature, the commission is not in a position to evaluate the results. the commission can only rely on the assessment carried out regularly by the united nations security council. the negotiations have now reached a turning point, as the deadline set by the parties themselves at the end of june 2002 could not be met. the accession negotiations with cyprus are proceeding as planned. the commission will present its regular reports on 16 october this year. the commission will also be presenting a report on the implementation of the eu's new strategy for the enlargement of the union. on the basis of these reports, the commission shall promote its recommendations to those countries which fulfil all the criteria for inclusion. these recommendations, together with the remaining questions arising from the negotiations, will be examined by the european council in brussels at the end of october. on the basis of these recommendations, the accession negotiations are to be concluded before the copenhagen european council in december 2002. the treaty of accession is due to be signed in spring 2003 and ratified by the national parliaments in the months to come. before the signature of the treaty of accession and in accordance with article 49 of the treaty on european union, the council, after consulting the commission and after obtaining the consent of the european parliament, shall decide on the admission of the candidate countries concerned.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply and for his position on this matter. i would just like to point out that we are a few weeks away from the commission reports and a few months away from the enlargement drive and there is still this big question mark over the republic of cyprus. i would like to assure the commissioner that while the european union and the commission will support all efforts to find a political solution, the failure to find a solution, which, as we know, is mainly due to the negative attitude of the turkish and turkish cypriot sides, will not be an obstacle to the participation of the republic of cyprus in the group of countries which will join the european union.
the commission is aware of the fact that the council has not yet taken a decision on the cyprus problem. the honourable member is also right to say that the european union is doing everything in its power to contribute to a solution. we will continue to do so. we will work until the last possible day to reach a solution. it is very difficult, mr president, to answer the question of the attitude of the turkish government, especially in the new political conditions now prevailing in turkey. i shall be having a conversation with the new turkish foreign minister later this week, and after that i shall perhaps know more about whether we can expect a decisive and constructive attitude from the turkish side in the period ahead. at the moment i have to tell you that i am no more able than you are to predict how turkey will behave on this issue, but i would like to take this opportunity to make a strong appeal to turkey and the turkish cypriots once again to realise that if we do not reach a solution to the cyprus problem, the ones who will suffer will be the turkish cypriots. they have the most to gain from a political solution and the accession of cyprus. but they also have the most to lose if a political solution is not found.
i am afraid, commissioner, that your answers have not provided anything new. it is well known that the inter-community talks are at an impasse and the prospects of resolving this impasse by december seem to be very slim. i would like to ask you two questions. firstly, as things stand, there is a clear risk of the island being divided, a risk of division, which will unfortunately confirm a prediction made by the communist party of greece, namely that the process of cyprus's accession to the european union would have such an effect. the question is, firstly, whether the commission has considered this possibility and what it has thought of doing to prevent it. secondly, we all know that the key to solving the cyprus problem is turkey. the impression is that the commission and the council, and the european union in general, have not exhausted all the possibilities of putting pressure on turkey. and of course you have raised the question of the impact of turkey's attitude, but i have the feeling that there could be even more pressure from the council and the commission. the question is whether there is a willingness to exert such pressure.
the commission is aware of the difficulties which the accession negotiations have created for cyprus. of the 31 chapters, 28 have been provisionally closed. that is all that is possible at this stage of the process. as regards the talks aimed at a political settlement under the auspices of the un secretary-general, i have just expressed - and the eu has repeatedly done so - its full support for this process. the commission's position on the question of how the dialogue between the two ethnic groups can influence the accession negotiations is determined by the conclusions of the helsinki european council in 1999 and the seville european council in 2002. the union has stated that it wishes to see the accession of a united island. however, it also stressed that a political solution is not a precondition for cyprus' accession to the union if the cyprus problem is not resolved by the end of the negotiations. the decision will be taken by the council taking into account all relevant factors. there is still a chance of achieving the objective of a political solution before accession. the european council therefore called on the leaders of the two cypriot groups to intensify the talks. if a political solution is found, the treaty of accession would reflect the agreement reached. as stated at the recent european council in seville, the european union is ready to incorporate the provisions of a comprehensive settlement for cyprus into the accession agreement. we have pointed out that we believe it is necessary that cyprus should be able to speak with one voice and that cyprus should be able to enforce the application of community law throughout cyprus. if a settlement is reached and cyprus joins as a single state, the european union is prepared to make a substantial financial contribution to support the rapid development of the northern part of the island in preparation for accession.
the commission's position on the accession of cyprus and the settlement of the cyprus problem is indeed constructive and positive, and i welcome that, commissioner. i would simply ask you to make your own recommendation to the council in october on the accession of the candidate countries. you said cyprus is making substantial progress. i think everyone also recognizes that the greek cypriot side has a good faith in dialogue, but there is a possibility that this dialogue, for reasons not related to the greek cypriot side, will fail. i would like the commission to make it clear what its recommendation will be in this case, namely how the possible failure of the intercommunal dialogue may affect the accession prospects of cyprus. that's my question.
mr president, i would like to ask you to give me a brief explanation of your position. of course, one could think of a long-term programming - five years - but, in our opinion, this period of time is excessive. if we do not talk about five years, the problems that the honourable member has raised will always exist. either it's a year or any number above a year poses those problems. however, i think their concern is that two or three years poses the same problem as four years. it's either five or it's one. five is too many, one makes us lose room for maneuver. in any case, i think it's a fake problem. because the necessary amendments can be discussed annually and therefore there is no difficulty, as i said to mr goebbels earlier, that a change of circumstances or an institutional change requires a re-examination of the problem; that can of course be done. secondly, what is the situation with ecofin employment and to what extent do these contradictions arise? what we are trying to avoid are contradictions in the process, for a very simple reason: the process starts with the commission, with a position which must be consistent; that position must be decided or endorsed on that basis by the european council; the ecofin councils and the employment council will have to work on the guidelines given by the spring european council, and it will be the june european council which will finally accept guidelines which must be consistent with each other and consistent with what the european council itself has put forward. consequently, such inconsistencies should not occur. one issue which i think is totally out of the question today is the stability pact. i won't take too long. the stability pact is in community law. my position is well known: i believe that the legislation must be complied with and therefore my position is that the stability pact must be applied as it is laid down in the regulations currently in force. this does not mean that the commission is not sensitive to the experience that the pact has given us and has not proposed - the commission itself - a number of interpretative elements in the light of economic developments, including such important elements as, for example, taking into account cyclically adjusted budgets in order to assess the reality of the possibility of achieving the nominal objectives envisaged in the stability programmes. i still think that the stability pact is an excellent instrument and that we must, of course, draw the best possible conclusions from reality in order to make the pact work better, but not in order to affect the substance of the pact. the last point is the legitimacy of the process raised by mrs randzio-plath. i believe that the process has all the legitimacy that is available at the present time; and i would say even more so, in so far as not only the sectoral councils are involved in these decisions, but also the european council at the beginning and at the end of the process and, secondly, not only the current commitment is maintained, or the current involvement of the european parliament, but attempts are made to increase it. the honourable member raises another problem: what kind of amendment to the treaty can be considered in the future for greater involvement of the european parliament in the process? this is not the subject of this communication. this is a matter which is pending before the convention, and the outcome will depend on the negotiations at the intergovernmental conference.
the commission. i would like to make three points, mr president. firstly, as has already been stressed, the whole proposal is within the framework of the existing treaty, without requiring any other or wider interpretation or change. secondly, this proposal is a step. we cannot say that we have solved the problem of the timing of the lisbon procedures. so, referring to what mrs van lancker said about other policies, such as the issue of pension schemes or other policies concerning social issues or the problems of exclusion that we have been discussing recently, i would like to say that these are all processes that are under way and obviously the aim is to get to a final coordination of the processes within a common framework. the third point which has been repeatedly discussed by the honourable members is how far in the medium or short term the timetable should be. i would say that the success of the proposal is that setting mediumterm objectives - i spoke about 2010 - is in fact very important because, particularly in employment, policies are structural and do not involve cyclical changes. so medium-term objectives are much more important and help governments to focus their efforts on implementing specific objectives. but at the same time we are proposing annual monitoring of the procedures, so there will be a debate in parliament on an annual basis on the basis of the reports that the member states will make and, as mr solbes mira said, when there are special events, exceptional circumstances, obviously there can be a review or new proposals.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. the commission's initiative for this proposal for a regulation is therefore welcome as an initiative, but not as a text, at least in some of its parts. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. what's that clause for? who does it serve, except a handful of large retail businesses that are no more than the fingers of one hand? no one at all! for the more than 20 million small and mediumsized businesses it creates deadly risks from unfair practices by large retailers who, by selling below cost, will be able to wipe out thousands of small and mediumsized businesses and minimise any potential competition they face. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's social policy is not distorted by the economic crisis. and more unemployed people are the last thing the currently struggling european economy could stand. but it is not serving consumers either, since the short-term opportunities for cheap products will be followed fairly soon by huge increases, the result of oligopolistic situations that will be created. so if we want to say that we represent millions of workers and entrepreneurs in the european union, if we want to say that small and mediumsized enterprises are the backbone of the european economy and society, if we want to continue to say that we are in touch with reality, then the sale of goods below cost must be voted down in tomorrow's vote.
i would also like to say, mr president, that it is important to harmonize sales promotion practices at community level, but the commission's proposals raise a number of questions. the most serious, as many colleagues have said, is the possibility of promoting sales below cost and the inability of the national authorities to restrict or prohibit them. i think that this does not contribute to healthy competition, it contributes to the promotion of unfair commercial practices, which are used by very large companies, it is not only directed against smaller companies, it is also directed against consumers because, once the periphery is destroyed, oligopolistic or monopolistic situations are formed. in this sense, i think it is very important that the legal affairs committee should intervene with the relevant amendments, which enable each member state to adopt or maintain relevant prohibition legislation. such legislation already exists in five countries of the european union, including greece, where law 2941 of 2001 is in force, which essentially safeguards the rules of competition by prohibiting sales below cost. the commission's text would make this law in breach of community law. the adoption of the amendment by the legal affairs committee of our parliament, i think, does not create any problems and that is why it is very important to support these amendments by the legal affairs committee.

in writing. - (el) the aim of this proposal for a commission regulation is to eliminate any restrictions imposed by member states on unfair sales promotions (price reductions outside the sales period, gifts, selling at a loss), such as the restrictions adopted by greece through law 2941/01 prohibiting selling at a loss. this will further help the big multinationals to raise money and take over the market, as only they will be able to increase their overall profits, even if they sell some products at a loss. at the same time, this will be catastrophic for small and mediumsized enterprises, which have already been hit hard by the implementation of the single internal market, and for consumers, who will be even more exposed to fraud and who, when markets are defined, will suffer the consequences. the fact that it has chosen a regulation, thus ensuring immediate application, and the fact that this proposal has not been discussed with stakeholders, especially smes, shows how much the eu wants to promote the interests of large companies. the whole case illustrates exactly what it means by competition in the single internal market. the european parliament's committee on budgets has already adopted a resolution on the subject.
in writing. - (el) state aid for employment is an important tool for stimulating the labour market and small and medium-sized enterprises. transparency and rules at european level are also essential. that said, i am concerned that the commission's regulation may deprive member state governments of additional, specific tools to combat unemployment in specific circumstances. the report also strongly advocates the 'flexibility' of the labour market, which could further weaken workers' employment rights, which is why i cannot vote for it.
in writing. - (de) i voted in favour of the berenguer fuster report because i support, like the rapporteur, the commission's approach to reforming the system of state aid for employment. i particularly welcome the fact that the planned limitation of the notification requirement for aid gives greater leeway to regional authorities in the development of local employment programmes, which will enable them to react, through targeted measures, quickly and flexibly to changes in the conditions of the regional labour market. it is mainly small and mediumsized enterprises which will benefit from the relaxation of the framework conditions, because they can expect quick and easy payment of state aid when needed, which i expressly support. by systematizing the existing legislation on state aid for employment into a single text, the commission is also pursuing its objective of greater transparency of european legislation. there is one point, however, on which i do not agree with the rapporteur. the key to improving employment cannot lie, as the rapporteur has suggested, in a relaxation of the labour market. it is rather a question of responding to changes in the labour market situation by rethinking employment policy and, for example, ensuring full employment through new working time models and at the same time ensuring higher quality jobs.
in writing. - (el) unemployment is rising steadily, followed even more rapidly by the rise in underemployment. the aim of eu employment policies is not to reduce unemployment; the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the problem of unemployment, and has already taken action to combat it. these state aids represent a net income for employers; the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem. they reduce labour costs and promote the interests and profits of large corporations. the priority for smes and the proposals to draw up a list of groups which will benefit from these measures simply make it possible to pass the pill. the aim of these proposals is not to help smes to strengthen their defenses against the onslaught of large companies, but to use smes, which have always represented the largest number of jobs, but which also have the trade union movements and the weakest social groups, to support this trend towards the flexibilization of the labour market. workers want stable, full-time employment, protected by social rights, a higher standard of living, real social progress and prosperity, unemployment benefits of 80% of the basic wage and a different policy, based on man and his needs.

mr president, first of all a comment on the way the president is responding. he may think the questioner's stupid. i want to assure him that we're not that stupid. the european union has a duty to ensure that the european union is able to take action in the fight against terrorism and to ensure that the european union is able to take action in the fight against terrorism. how can he answer us like that ? what are we ?
the council. - i have to answer question no 6 by mr carlos lage. as regards the first question, i would like to point out that neither article 33 of the treaty on european union nor article 64 (1) of the ec treaty allows the council to intervene in the maintenance of public policy and public security in the member states. this also applies to measures relating to public policy taken by member states when european council meetings are held on their territory. so the community cannot interfere. as regards the second question, i would refer to the decision of the executive committee of 20 december 1995 on the procedure for applying article 2 (2) of the convention implementing the schengen agreement, which has a long reference number which i will not read out. this decision lays down detailed rules on when checks at internal borders may be reintroduced. it forms part of the so-called schengen rules and is therefore binding on all schengen states. it was published in oj l 239, 22.9.2000, p. 133.
the council. - as has been said, it is not for the council to decide when it is appropriate to use the schengen rules to temporarily close borders. i can inform you that the danish presidency reserves the right to use this opportunity. we have said so explicitly, but we are pleased that on the weekend just ended there were so few demonstrators at the foreign ministers' summit in helsingør that there were twice as many policemen as demonstrators. let us hope that this sets a trend for the future.
it is not the first time that the unacceptable phenomenon of citizens of one member state being prevented from crossing the border into another member state and being mistreated has occurred, and this is particularly the case with demonstrators against the policy of the european union. the european union has been a major player in the fight against terrorism, and it is not just the european union that is responsible for this, but also the european union itself, which is responsible for the whole of europe. this is what happened to the danish protesters, your fellow countrymen, mr president, at the gothenburg summit, when they wanted to come over and join the protesters against the policy of the european union. we see that schengen was not created, despite the declarations, to facilitate the free movement of citizens of the european union's member states within the european union, but to turn the european union into a fortress area. it applies when it is in the interests of the european union's ruling circles to allow the free movement of capital, and it does not apply when it is a question of the movement of protesters against the interests of capital and the policy of the european union.
commission - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, let me first of all thank the committee on culture, youth, education, the media and sport, and particularly its rapporteur, for their commitment and also for the constructive criticisms which have been made. this report confirms, if need be, the importance which parliament attaches to our action in the field of lifelong learning. i also welcome the fact that parliament supports the general principles which have been underlined by the commission in its communication. in particular, the broad objectives of lifelong learning and the need for every citizen, i said every citizen, to have access to education and training throughout life. we know that we are far from being able to do this: only 8% of european adults have access to lifelong learning and almost two in ten young people leave school without a qualification. this is an intolerable situation common to all our member states. i am relieved to see that, as we get back to school, most ministers are dealing with this problem, which is already a scandal. the solution to these two problems - education and lifelong learning - requires the involvement of all the actors concerned and the necessary financial resources. mr president, parliament is addressing many points in its report. i would like to highlight three of them: basic skills, investment and the european area of lifelong learning. as regards basic skills, our objective is clear: every european must have these skills to be able to learn, work and live in the knowledge society. i say "live", because it is also about personal development and the ability to play a role in our society, in our democracies. i think particularly of the more disadvantaged who, like me, must learn to read, write and count. it is well known that illiteracy remains a major problem and that the main obstacle to social integration, as mrs hermange's report pointed out, is the lack of basic knowledge. i would also add to this basic knowledge the "digital divide" between those who have access to new technologies and those who do not. finally, let me return to one of my main problems: the lack of language skills and the lack of personal and social skills. the member states and the commission are currently working on the topic of basic skills in the context of the implementation of the work programme on the objectives of education and training systems. together we have identified the necessary core competences and there will be an exchange of good practice to see together what works, what does not work, what can be taken up by one or the other, what needs to be developed by all, and the conclusions of this work will be incorporated in the interim report to be sent by the education council and the commission to the european council in spring 2004. mr president of the committee on culture, as promised, your committee will be involved in this work. secondly, the financing. this is a sensitive subject, as you know, and it is primarily the responsibility of the member states. it is clear that if we are to establish a genuine lifelong learning system, if we are to translate our political ambitions into concrete action, more will have to be spent, not only by the member states but also by the social partners. it is true, madam rapporteur, that when it comes to lifelong learning, we are moving beyond the formal to the informal, and that means that we are associating with partners who are not traditionally in school. the first step is therefore to define national strategies. in this respect, the member states have made considerable progress, especially those which were lagging behind the most. the only thing left now is to strengthen the coherence of the strategies - a coherence which, i must say quite openly, is lacking most of the time - and to translate these strategies into concrete action. this is where we need to develop the partnerships you highlight in your report. because lifelong learning is a shared responsibility, national strategies must be accompanied by agreements at all levels, including at local level, and between all actors, in particular the social partners. unfortunately, this is done very unevenly and far too timidly. thirdly, the european area. while the responsibility for implementing these policies lies primarily with the member states, the community itself has a role to play in creating a european area of lifelong learning. a space in which citizens, whoever they are and from whichever country they come, can use their skills and qualifications from one country to another, whether to study, train, work, engage or live. we know, and we have discussed this on several occasions, that there are still many obstacles to the creation of such a space, particularly as regards the recognition of qualifications. however, there has been a great deal of progress in this area, and you can be sure, ladies and gentlemen, that the commission is working on this, so that we can achieve the recognition of qualifications and, especially in the field of vocational training, we can open up new perspectives and engage in new methods of working together. a political initiative has been taken to strengthen cooperation between member states in the field of vocational training. it is generally called the bruges process, because it was in the beautiful city of bruges that the ministers and the commission decided to go in this direction. projects are being carried out, such as that of former french minister mélenchon, who is a model of the kind. new initiatives are being taken, progress is being made and i must say that, despite all the difficulties that have arisen, governments are moving in the direction described because they have understood the absolute necessity for it. at the next education council in november, a resolution must be adopted. it will also be discussed by the governments of the thirty when they meet at the end of the year. we will therefore extend the issue of lifelong learning to our future partners in the european union. i would like to say this: my services are implementing the communication. a european portal on apprenticeship opportunities will be launched before the end of the year. we will be launching other actions in the coming weeks, such as the creation of european networks of learning regions and the setting up of a database of good practice. all this will be very concrete because we have analysed the problems, we know them. we know what we have to do, so let's do it! (applause) i would like to thank
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has adopted a number of amendments to the proposal for a council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the permissible sound power level of towed vehicles. i think we can all agree that lifelong learning is an important policy tool and i would like to congratulate mrs van brempt on not hesitating to speak very important truths. because despite our agreements, despite the fact that we all recognise that training and lifelong learning must be linked to the world of work in order to be effective, there is a problem and a gap. it is based on education, but we have very big problems here and training cannot replace inadequate education. we have great disparities between the member states in the field of education, this is recognised in the report, and therefore we have great gaps and great problems in lifelong training too. it is characteristic that we have not yet managed to have an agreement on what the basic skills in education are. every time the oecd data for the different countries are published, we see that they cause a lot of upset and there are countries that do not accept so easily the criteria that have been used. the commission has also taken note of the need to modify and improve education systems. but what are we doing about it as the european union? we're trying to coordinate, but we're not going any deeper. and i think that's a very big political shortcoming. what are the member states also doing about their lifelong learning strategy? we have at least one-half of the member states without an explicit strategy, which is nevertheless so necessary in order to have economic growth, to have improvement in industry and mobility, to have improvement in unemployment programmes. i think that the determination of expenditure by the member states would be a very necessary element. lifelong learning should also be able to appeal to the underprivileged, the unskilled, those with shorter periods of study, the unemployed and, of course, migrants.
the committee on women's rights and equal opportunities has adopted an opinion on the proposal for a directive on the protection of women against discrimination. madam president, commissioner, there is no doubt that in the new circumstances created by globalisation and the rapid development of science and technology, the continuous renewal of knowledge through lifelong learning is a prerequisite for the best possible use of human resources. in this sense, we urgently need a european policy to give impetus to this sector, and in this respect i congratulate you, commissioner, on your initiatives, and our rapporteur on her excellent report. as the author of the opinion of the committee on women's rights, i would like to stress that such a policy of lifelong learning can only exist if it takes seriously into account the new reality created by the massive and dynamic entry of women into the labour market. women are now the most dynamic factor in growth, since, as you know, two thirds of the new jobs that are estimated to be created in the next decade will be filled by women. the gender dimension must therefore be taken seriously into account in the formulation of all policies and measures must be implemented to eliminate direct or indirect discrimination against women, which ultimately comes at the expense of sustainable development and the competitiveness of the economy. an active lifelong learning policy should be designed with a focus on women's potential, i.e. women's participation, skills enhancement, broadening of their professional options, participation in decision-making processes, gender balance in the fields of science and new technologies, and elimination of the gender segregation of the labour market. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area. this digital divide is mainly about women. special attention should also be paid to the most vulnerable groups of women, such as migrant and rural women, as well as older women. last but not least, gender mainstreaming policy, as well as work-life balance, should be part of the overall lifelong learning policy.
the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of teaching and to promote the use of the european language. their basic functions are being privatised, they are being increasingly controlled by business interests, they are being forced to operate as enterprises under competitive and, more generally, market conditions. the result is an overall deterioration in the quality of the knowledge provided. the european union is the main force pushing in this direction and the report follows the same line. the communist party of greece is radically opposed to the bologna process, to the operation of private universities, to the interventions through the green paper and the so-called european higher education area and, of course, to the creation of a european university headquarters, which abolishes autonomy and the free movement of ideas. we are fighting for a public university, under academic control and not a corporate entity, which will respond to popular and social needs. we support cooperation between universities with respect for the academic order. ladies and gentlemen, the university is not a grocery store. if we allow it to happen, we will destroy any hope of young people to gain comprehensive knowledge and any hope of society for a better future.

in writing. - (el) lifelong learning (not apprenticeship) policy is a policy designed to replace systematic education and basic learning with a superficial, market-oriented transfer of skills and practices with its own deadline for sale, and whose primary objective is to meet the demand of large enterprises for flexible, flexible workers, to restructure labour relations and to promote the capacity for vocational integration. it is a way of putting ideological and psychological pressure on workers by making them bear the responsibility for unemployment, accepting longterm unemployment and erasing the link between education and job prospects. at the same time, traditional schools are becoming central offices for lifelong learning, "opening their establishments" to "local communities and businesses". in other words, multinationals will have a say in school curricula, as is already the case in eu-funded pilot projects in greece. we radically oppose this policy and we fight for the abolition of any form of discrimination or obstacle to equal access for all young people to free, standardised and state-funded education up to the age of 18, the kind of education that is essential for further professional specialization, an education that will help young people to put their ideas into practice, to equip themselves with scientific criteria in relation to nature and society and to become conscientious contributors to social progress. that is why the members of the communist party of greece voted against the van brempt report.

mr president, commissioner, it is clear that the events of 11 september and the war on terror have served as an alibi for many member states of the european union to block the process of an integrated european asylum policy. the number of asylum seekers in the european union has fallen by almost a third in the first four months of 2002 compared with the same period last year, and has almost halved in the decade. in greece, a country with a general respect for asylum, grants for the first seven months of 2002 amounted to no more than 59 out of a total of 2810 applications. this is a dramatic decrease from 2001, when the rate of recognition was almost tenfold. at the same time, data from the united nations high commissioner for refugees show that neighbouring countries - also poor - offer shelter to the largest part of the refugee population, while few, if any, turn to the west. it is significant that out of the 4 million afghan refugees, only 38 600 asylum applications were submitted to the union last year, i.e. only one in every 1 000 afghans sought protection from the european union, while the majority of them took refuge in neighbouring states. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. at the same time, of course, there is a growing hostility towards foreigners in the european union, and the debate is moving away from the causes that drive these people away from their homes and the human rights violations in their countries of origin. the strict control measures not only do not prevent asylum seekers from reaching the union, but also lead many to enter illegally and inevitably cooperate with trafficking networks. the result is that we are counting the dead on europe's borders and coasts and that new forms of slavery and over-exploitation are emerging. we agree with the open method of coordination, commissioner, but it is not a complete solution to the problem. we hope to have a comprehensive policy by the end of 2003.

in writing. - (el) as today's debate has shown, the machinery for producing undemocratic and repressive legislation has been set in motion in the european union. the aim of the spanish government's initiatives (voted without debate, under the new regulation) is to strengthen and speed up the decisions already taken on the collection and exchange of information and to put the member states under the yoke of the strictest supervision to ensure that they quickly and faithfully transpose european legislation on terrorism and the european arrest warrant, as if there were a fear that these member states - yielding to the pressure of democratic citizens' movements which are already expressing their opinion in various countries - would not bow to this infamous legislation. in the context of enhanced cooperation, member states are required, in defiance of any notion of national sovereignty, to collect a minimum of facts on alleged terrorist offences and to transmit them to europol and eurojust. what cooperation and the standardisation of police and judicial cooperation really means is clearly shown by a confidential europol document which has recently been leaked and which, under the evocative title 'european best practices in the treatment of information sources', sanctions the use of fraud and whistleblowing by police officers, actions which will remain unpunished even if the officers concerned have participated in the crime. that is why the members of the greek communist party voted against the texts in question.
in writing. - (el) the commission proposal on transboundary movements of genetically modified organisms is unfortunately not as far as the cartagena protocol which it is supposed to implement. the following are the main shortcomings to be addressed. firstly, the precautionary principle must be clearly stated in the community text. secondly, the same criteria should be applied to the eu's internal market and external trade. if a product is not marketed on the domestic market, it should also be prohibited for export. thirdly, the exporting country should take full responsibility for the movement of banned gmos and not pass them on to the importing country. fourth, the public must be fully informed. fifthly, we must do everything possible to ensure that large companies do not use their multinational structure to evade controls. the amendments proposed by the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy of the european parliament are in the right direction, and that is why i support them.
in writing. - (el) the report by mrs roberta angelilli improves the commission's green paper on a legislative framework for compensation for victims of crime. the basic idea put forward in the report that all member states should establish compensation schemes for victims of crime - including victims of terrorism - who cannot obtain compensation from the offender or through other means is a good idea, given that the european convention on compensation for victims of violent crimes, which has been in force since 1988, has not been signed by all member states and that, even where it applies, it does not allow victims of violent crimes to seek compensation from the state. the directive on best practice in the various member states, which the commission is also invited to introduce, is a good initiative which will help the less experienced member states to deal with these issues.
mr. president, the stock markets have collapsed, forever cancelling the supposed miracle of the new economy and sucking the savings of working people into the hands of capital. the threat of war in iraq, in addition to its inhuman character, is driving up the price of oil, adding to inflationary pressures. the results of the economy, and especially of industrial production, are the least disappointing. capitalism is in deep crisis. the only thing that remains is the obsession with the stability pact, the anti-worker policy and the attack on workers' rights, living standards and pensions, the reduction of farmers' incomes and the strangulation of small and mediumsized enterprises. the european union's 2003 budget is another practical step in implementing this anti-people policy. instead of redistributing income, which represents less than 1.01% of the community's gdp, it is further strengthening monopolies and imperialist schemes to oppress and exploit the people. the agricultural economy is a typical example, where community funds not only do not prevent the income of poor and middle-class farmers from being reduced and their land from being cleared, but through the system of prices and subsidies they ensure cheap raw materials for tobacco and other manufacturers and increase their profits. the common agricultural policy has created a large deficit in the european union's trade balance, amounting to eur 25 billion, while jobs are being lost and unemployment is rising. the objectives of big business for 2003 are made clear by the relative reduction in the funds proposed at a time when people's needs are increasing, unemployment is rising, the environment is being destroyed, so-called globalisation is creating new forms of exploitation and is multiplying poverty and hunger in the world. the reduction in the budget for structural policies, the reduction in agricultural expenditure and the allocation of resources for the common foreign, defence and security policy show the aggressiveness of capital against the peoples and the role which the european union is called upon to play as the assistant of the world policeman, the united states. we are opposed to this policy and the methods of big business. we reject the choices of the monopolies, we fight together with the workers to overthrow these plans and we demand another policy, which will lead to the economic well-being of the workers and the safeguarding of their rights.
mr president, we are at the end of the debate, and i think we can all agree that the employment strategy has not been successful everywhere, or at least not in the same way. the important point, however, is that all countries are now on a path of change in national policy making, notably in shifting the priority from managing unemployment to managing employment growth. the report by mr schmid highlights a number of issues which our committee voted in favour of and which are intended to complement and enrich the european commission's communication in order to strengthen this strategy which has been launched in luxembourg. i would like to focus my speech on a number of issues which have been raised by other colleagues this evening. firstly, the involvement of local and regional authorities and non-governmental organisations is not satisfactory. the commissioner mentioned that there are local action plans which have been submitted and implemented by local actors and which can be examples to follow, but i think they are not the norm. i believe, as mr andersson said, that we must study what these examples are and really look at the conditions and circumstances under which some regions benefited and others did not. i will tell you about something that amazes me in my country, greece. we are in an election period for local and regional elections and nowhere is the role that local bodies can play in creating jobs highlighted. all of them are promoting their role in maintaining cleanliness, creating green spaces, some expressions of social policy, but never that they can mobilize local forces and create jobs. as for the role of non-governmental organisations, they are in an even worse situation and unless their place in a social dialogue is institutionalised, i do not think they will be able to be serious and effective partners in local society. another issue is women's unemployment - the rapporteur for the committee on women, mr mann, made a special reference to this - and i believe that even there, despite the increases in jobs that exist, the member states must show great commitment and consistency in adopting quantitative targets for the qualitative upgrading of women's work, but also the creation of new jobs. this is directly linked to the issue raised tonight, and i strongly support it, the need to coordinate employment policy with social policy. the commission has already taken a number of initiatives to improve the quality of life of the elderly, and it is hoped that the commission will be able to take this into account in its proposals for the future. this is a matter of social policy, which is the responsibility of the member states, but also of the question of social responsibility which has been raised in this parliament and which is the responsibility of the social partners. we therefore see that a great effort must be made to make the employment strategy multi-level and not to forget the serious role that national and regional parliaments - wherever they exist - have to play, because employment policy is really not a matter of serious concern to them.
the commission. mr president, i would first of all like to thank mrs smet for this report, which shows that very little progress has been made in the area of the participation of both sexes in the social partners and that discrimination is alive and well. it is terrible to see that in the very large trade union organizations of europe, with hundreds of thousands of members, there is not even an indication of representation of the two sexes and that we have entire boards of directors of trade unions where they are all men. while we have an increase in women in the labour market, this is not even in proportion to their participation in trade unions, which is on average below 25%, while women's participation is above 40%. the situation is even worse for employers, as mrs smet said, since the lowest rate is found in belgium at 1.5% and the highest in finland, but only at 19%. of course, we can see in recent years an increased activity of women, but i will remind you of the conclusion of beijing 10 years ago, that if we continue at this rate we will reach a balanced participation in 2500 years. what can we do at european level? it is clearly not a matter that can be resolved by legislation or binding measures. the commission, as already mentioned, has chosen the surveyor to monitor and record statistics throughout europe, because today we have only the national representations, we have no sectoral analytical representation, nor the analysis by member state. this statistical tracing will take four years, but already in the first year we will be able to use the data, and it will help us in particular to see in which areas we should intervene by networking, by sharing practices, by cooperation, by incentives in the unions. the second is that we need to agree on specific quantitative targets and timetables for women's participation. the social partners, who are going to present their joint agenda at christmas, intend, as far as i know, to include the issue of equality among the common issues they will try to resolve in the coming years. so if the social partners at european level are to present a common agenda at christmas and put the issue of gender equality on the agenda, it is very important that we move forward on specific quantitative targets and timetables. the commission itself continues to seek a balanced participation of women and men at all levels and in the committees it sets up, but it must be said that the commission's trade unions have minimal participation of women. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work is a step in the right direction, and it is a step in the right direction. we believe that the employment strategy and the social dialogue can indeed make some progress, but the commission cannot push things forward on its own unless the social partners themselves take on a large part of the responsibility, and that is why their own initiative is needed here too.
mr president, i too would like to congratulate mrs smet on her initiative in drawing up this report and bringing to light a subject which has been more or less forgotten at european and national level. it is true that there is little information on the underrepresentation of women in the social partners' organisations and on their lack of participation in the negotiations on social dialogue. it is also true that the deficit, this problem, is not recognized at the political level. indeed, while the absence of women from parliaments, governments, local and regional authorities is often highlighted, there is little mention of the absence of women from the social partners. we must therefore be convinced, as european institutions, as well as national governments and the social partners, that the participation of women in decision-making processes within the social partners will not only give legitimacy to power and quality to democracy, but will ultimately also promote our development objectives, because it will contribute to increasing employment, improving the quality of work, increasing productivity and competitiveness. as for women's participation in decision-making, it is the only way to take account of the demands of women, workers and the unemployed, to develop social responsibility for the benefit of women in work, to create the conditions for the development of creativity in the workplace, to take account of the specific characteristics of women, to tackle the wage gap, which is the most basic of all. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done.

- doc. b5-0491/02 by mr de gucht, on behalf of the group of the european people's party, on the revision of trademark law.
commission. – i should like to thank the members who have spoken on this subject very much for their views. i shall start with mr harbour, who has in his usual eloquent manner impressed the commission with the need to change over to international recognition. in his closing remarks he said that he wants the commission, and this commissioner in particular, to see that consumers get the benefits to which he says they are entitled so that there can be a true single market. i should like to tell mr harbour that we already have a single market. that market is confined to the european union. it is not something that appertains to the whole world. the commission is aware that consumers benefit from lower prices. that is obvious. the commission is very much in favour of seeing to it that consumers benefit from prices which are as low as possible. however, they are not the only ones involved. it is not just consumers who are interested in this matter. producers are also involved and, in particular, trademark holders. just as consumers are entitled to the lowest prices that are economically feasible, so the producers of trademarked goods want and are entitled to get protection for their trademarks – but not unlimited protection. that is the reason behind exhaustion in the european union. there should certainly be a balance between, on the one hand, the interests of the consumer and, on the other hand, the interests of the producer, and the commission continues to believe that european exhaustion provides for that balance. the commission has not changed its mind since we last spoke about this issue 12 months ago. i accept mr mayer's apologies. he said the studies are of insufficient quality. i do not accept that. i do not understand why the nera study should be of insufficient quality. the nera study, as is well known because it is available, states that the changeover from european to international exhaustion would have a negligible effect on prices. mr mayer may know that in his country, germany, there was once a system of international exhaustion. because of the policy accepted in germany on account of the european union, the changeover was made from international exhaustion to european exhaustion. it was then claimed that certain luxury goods producers had to raise their prices, but we then heard from those luxury goods producers that they had in fact lowered their prices. so, in that specific case, international exhaustion did not produce prices any lower than european exhaustion – quite the contrary. mr mayer may have in mind the studies carried out by the uk and swedish governments two years ago. those studies – interesting as they are – showed that there is a great disparity in prices between the different countries belonging to the european union. what stuck most in my mind was the 40% difference between electronic goods sold in sweden and those sold in germany, which are almost neighbours, apart from the stretch of water between them. a 40% difference in price means that the internal market is not working as it should, because, if it did, prices should be related to objective costs – costs of transport, distribution, etc. but 40% means that other factors come into play. 40% is far and away bigger than any differences in prices that come into play and are discussed in comparisons between international exhaustion and european exhaustion. so i am afraid that the studies of which the commission is aware – the nera study was commissioned by the commission itself – do not support the view that international exhaustion would lead to lower prices, quite apart from the fact that, as i have said before, the rightholders of trademarks are entitled to proper protection, not unlimited protection, but substantial nevertheless. i thank mrs fourtou very much for supporting the nera study. she has cited the united states and japan. the commission agrees with her view. as to her question on the madrid protocol, i must tell her that this is in the hands of the council, and the commission can only wait for the council to take a positive decision in this area. once again, the report will deal with matters of abuse of trademarks. as i have said, the commission has sent out 60 letters to companies and legal bodies asking for evidence of abuses of trademark rights. the answers should be in by the end of september. we do not have all the answers yet. we shall wait for them to come in. we shall analyse them. they will be included in the report due at the end of december. i should like to confirm that parliament will indeed receive that report at the end of december. i should like to conclude by stressing that the commission has an open mind on this issue. only those who have no ideas never change them. the commission is full of ideas and has an open mind. it is always willing to reconsider. as i said a year ago, the problem is more a lack of definitive evidence showing that there is an advantage in opting for international exhaustion over european exhaustion. i do not believe that the commission should change its policy without clear evidence of there being an advantage in doing so. with these remarks, i should like to end my response in winding up this debate. once again, there will be a report at the end of december. i look forward to a further exchange of views with parliament once that report has been brought to the attention of this house.

the commission is not prepared to accept this. madam president, the report on the work of the committee on petitions for 2001-2002, of which i have the honour of being rapporteur, and the corresponding annual report on the activities of the european ombudsman, which are being discussed together today, should not be considered as two routine reports. there are very specific reasons for this. these reports refer to two fundamental rights, the right of reference and the right to complain, which are linked to the citizenship of the european union and are the main means of strengthening the political control of the european parliament over the place and extent of the application of community law by the community institutions and the member states. the report and the complaints give us a clear picture of the extent to which the european citizen's expectations of europe are being met. the desire to bring europe closer to its citizens, the promise to strengthen the democratic legitimacy of the european union, and in particular of the european parliament, and the much-vaunted objective of greater transparency can find their true expression, to a large extent, through the substantial development of the institutions of referral and complaint. in view of this, i would ask my colleagues to study these reports and the proposals carefully, all of which are designed to increase the credibility of the community institutions and the effectiveness of the measures taken. many of these proposals can be implemented within the framework of the existing treaties. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work he has done. in both cases, these issues must be given priority in the activities of the political groups. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will environmental issues, as well as issues of recognition of diplomas and qualifications. it is encouraging that in many cases, after cooperation with the relevant national authorities, those who express not only my congratulations but my admiration for his work to date, which justifies the institution of mediation in the eyes of the european citizen and leaves a great legacy for its preservation and strengthening in the future.
mr president, while i too congratulate the european ombudsman on his truly excellent and valuable work, i would like to refer in particular to the excellent report by my dear colleague, mr koukiadis, and to focus the house's attention in particular on two points which in my view should be included in the treaties on this occasion and in the convention. the first concerns point 17, which calls for ways of pressuring national authorities to respond to complaints from their citizens and to questions raised by the committee on petitions and the commission. often we encounter indifference or we encounter inaction. the rapporteur and the committee on petitions unanimously adopted the following proposal which i have the honour to put forward: in the event of negligence or obstruction by the national authorities, the national media should be informed of this negative behaviour and the content of the complaint lodged by the complainants. nothing is known to move and disturb national and local authorities more than the publicity of their illegal acts or omissions or even their indifference to their citizens. this publicity can be achieved by sending out press releases in the national language and by holding press conferences in which national meps, and in particular the members of the committee on petitions, could also take part. the second point i would like to stress is that: a number of reports received by the committee on petitions report on planned or ongoing projects or behaviour by national authorities which breach mandatory european union rules, often resulting in irreparable damage to the environment or european cultural heritage. the procedures leading to a conviction by the european court of justice are so time-consuming that they prove to be useless when the damage has already been done and can no longer be repaired. i therefore believe that we should look for ways of preventing the evil until the final judgment of the european court of justice, since that report has been referred to. in national legislation, it is well known that temporary safeguard or conservation measures, which immediately stop the start or continuation of a destructive activity until its legality has been determined, have a deterrent effect. the commission has already made a number of proposals in this area, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. why else, what can happen when a habitat is destroyed, for example? it can't be rebuilt.
