mr president, the kyoto protocol has been hailed by many as an important step towards tackling the climate change that threatens life on our planet. however, after pressure from the us, the protocol included a loophole that allows states and companies to opt out of their specific obligations, and that is the greenhouse gas emissions trading system. it is unacceptable to create a whole market which will trade pollution as a commodity and where, instead of some firms reducing some of the emissions they cause, they will buy the surplus of others, which have not exceeded their limit. it is equally unacceptable to present pollution as a right. combined with the infamous "polluter pays" principle, this means that anyone who can pay has a license to pollute unchecked. we deplore the fact that the european union is supporting its policy of applying the kyoto protocol to trade in pollutants and that it is handling the whole problem on market terms. this shows how hypocritical her declarations about the environment and the climate are. the commission is therefore proposing to extend the scope of the kyoto protocol to include the use of biofuels. the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions is incompatible with the maintenance of the profits of the monopolies. of course, under capitalism everything is sold and everything is bought, even pollution. but the climate, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, and the future of life on our planet are not for sale or for purchase. his protection is in the hands of the popular movement.
mr president, mr president of the commission, ladies and gentlemen, the stability and growth pact was introduced in 1997 and is an extension of the excessive deficit procedure, the avoidance of which was one of the famous maastricht conditions for the admission of a member state to the single currency. the european council in madrid, in the context of the european council in madrid, has made it clear that the european union is not prepared to accept the maastricht treaty as a basis for a new stability pact. many people may feel that the introduction and success of the euro has ex post facto justified the very restrictive conditions on inflation and the budget deficit. it's wrong! the strict restrictive conditions of maastricht have certainly succeeded in keeping prices down, but they have also made the unemployment situation in the european economy worse. unemployment and recessions have undermined the value of the euro, not strengthened it. the fact that the euro has lost almost 20% of its value against the dollar since its introduction, at a time when budget deficits were better controlled than they are today, and now that deficits are supposed to be getting out of hand, the euro is at almost one to one with the dollar. it also shows that whenever the european central bank raised interest rates, i.e. tightened monetary policy, the value of the euro fell instead of rising, as those who raised interest rates expected. the financial markets, mr president, are supposed to punish europe for the indiscipline of its governments by devaluing the euro. that's wrong! the financial markets are well aware of the importance of high economic activity for the value of a currency and do not seem to be prepared to punish a reasonable relaxation of restrictive policy - logical of course because no one is talking about excesses and irresponsibilities - at a time when we are on the verge of a new great economic recession. i believe that this analysis, mr president, justifies the characterization and analysis of mr prodi.

mr president, i would like to thank the two rapporteurs for the excellent work they have brought to this debate, and in particular mrs grossetête with whom we have had a very productive and good cooperation. as regards the proposal for a revision of the pharmaceutical legislation, which aims to ensure a high level of public health, to complete the internal market and the pharmaceutical sector, to meet the challenges of enlargement, to introduce a more functional and simplified system of authorization and supervision of medicinal products, and indeed at affordable prices, and finally to ensure transparency and dissemination of information to patients about medicinal products, i should like to touch briefly on the following points. innovation: without scientific research and innovation, there can be no synthesis of new substances and medicines that will make a decisive contribution to the prevention and treatment of diseases. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's research and development programmes are properly coordinated and that the results of the research are available to the member states. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment. the cost of developing the drug is therefore covered by the market and social security systems, which are now under tremendous pressure, also due to the ageing of the population. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's pharmaceutical industry is not left behind by the introduction of new technologies. i believe, however, that the arrangements which have been made in the regulation and which are now being proposed ensure both its exclusivity and its reasonable duration. the safety of pharmaceutical products is also a major concern. quality, vigilance are also elements taken into account in the excellent report. i am in favour of a centralised procedure, mr president, with a certain decentralised procedure for the member states. finally, subsidiarity: this is a very important element, because it is the member states that are responsible for the financing and provision of health care. we saw yesterday that president bush made a move precisely because the economic pressure on member states is very great in this area.
commission. – mr president, today's debate has been very instructive and shows the great commitment of many honourable members of this house to these issues. a number of interesting issues have been raised. i want to tackle those raised by members who are still here in the chamber. it is unfortunate that often members have disappeared when it is time for me to reply – i hope this is not an improper comment. i will comment on six groups of amendments. firstly, on the extension of the scope of the centralised procedure. mrs grossetête put the whole case very well. the whole idea behind this procedure is to guarantee that goods are delivered quickly, equitably and in the interest of all patients in europe. if we do not have a centralised procedure by the time the ten new member states join, there will be enormous inequality. we take the comment on the smes very seriously and, for that reason, we are ready to seek concrete measures that would alleviate the burden on smes, for instance, a reduction in fees, direct scientific advice from the agency in london or administrative support. the commission is also convinced that extended scope for the centralised procedure for human and veterinary medicine would benefit european citizens and businesses alike. for that reason, the commission cannot accept amendments which reduce the scope of the centralised procedure. the second question is the balance between innovation and competition from generics. that is a very sensitive issue. i would be happy to discuss that in depth. in regard to the american system, as mrs corbey mentioned, we must remember that we cannot take just one point of comparison. in america, the drugs market is totally deregulated and there is direct consumer advertising. we are not proposing that. it is through data protection that we are trying to strike a balance. we are proposing something that the commission refused to propose for years – namely the bola system for generics which, of course, means more competition in the sector after the expiry of a patent and, at the same time, lower prices and lost revenue for those who originated the medicine. it is clearly better for the consumer. it is extremely important that we stick to that. secondly, at the same time, to counterbalance it, we want to propose a solution for exclusivity involving a ten-year period and, if there are indications needing to be researched into later, ten plus one. i do not believe that european industries have been losing market share, that there are less innovative medicines and research in this field, and that this is in the interests of european patients. i just do not believe it. i am sure that we must try to accept that there are two or three public goods we can defend at the same time. public health takes top priority but we must also support research innovation in europe. if we do not support it we are going to be in permanent decline in all sectors. research-intensive areas are those which create jobs, development and wealth and make it possible to assume our global responsibilities. so both are issues we must try to tackle at the same time. the balance is not always easy. we have done this after very careful consideration. there are a few amendments seeking to change this and we will oppose them. for instance, amendment no 34 wants to go further in favour of generics and weaken the position of innovation. to keep the balance, we cannot support it. on the other hand, amendments nos 40 and 92 call for additional data protection of three years in two situations. firstly, when a new indication is developed for an old medicine, and secondly, when a company submits documentation for switching a product from prescription to non-prescription status. these amendments must be seen in the whole context of the data-protection scheme. as regards the additional data protection on switching, we agree that this request is not unjustified. this could increase the availability of over-the-counter medicines. we can therefore accept amendment no 92 in principle, but we still retain our right to look at the duration of that additional protection. as far as the additional data protection for old products is concerned, this entails a proposal that could extend data protection far beyond ten years – up to 14 years, to be precise. that is why we cannot support amendment no 40. the third issue concerns improving information for patients. i feel a little uncomfortable with this debate because i am also against the proposal that many members oppose, namely the american model. for instance, one member said that in america the ten most used pharmaceuticals are the ones that are the most advertised. among the five most advertised medicines at least, no one would have a right to take part in even the pilot project. a pilot project does not mean direct advertising. it is only information validated by the competent authorities which can be given at the request of the patient or patients' organisations. it is right that people are against the american direct-to-consumer advertising. that is not what we are proposing. we have a situation where abundant information is available on the web, which is not validated and makes the consumer extremely unsure, which concentrates on products which are not on the market and which confuses many citizens when they visit their doctors. because we cannot stop it, we want to offer the possibility of access to information validated by clear guidelines, accepted by the community and monitored, observed and controlled by the medicines evaluation agency. i understand that people are against the american system, but we have never proposed it. that is why we cannot support amendment no 101 and others aiming at the same objective. the fourth issue concerns relative effectiveness. some amendments relate to relative effectiveness. they suggest that the added therapeutic value should be considered as a criterion for the authorisation of a medicinal product – alongside quality, safety and efficacy. the commission agrees that the issue of relative effectiveness is of great importance because it can help the kind of innovation that brings important benefits to patients. an authorisation depends on a positive benefit/risk ratio, on the quality, safety and efficacy of the medicine itself, on its own merit. relative effectiveness is not a reason to deny access to the market. the commission can support the idea that relative effectiveness should be considered at eu level, and can accept in principle some related amendments, like amendments nos 4 and 100. the commission cannot, however, accept amendments that establish relative effectiveness as a fourth hurdle for marketing authorisation. the fifth group of amendments concerns legal status. the commission proposes that the conditions of supply – over-the-counter or prescription-only – should be determined under the mutual recognition procedure. this would obviously lead to a harmonised eu legal status for all products authorised accordingly. the commission continues to believe that it is appropriate to include legal status in the mutual recognition procedure. but the commission understands the concerns that this might lead to a situation where too many products are classified as prescription-only medicines because of a 'lowest common denominator' effect among the member states. finally, on the european medicines evaluation agency, our proposal follows the lines which were provided for the european food safety authority at the time of our proposal. amendment no 16 aligns the emea management board with the model finally adopted for the food safety authority, and the commission can support this amendment. finally, let me just briefly reiterate that parliament is provided in writing with a full list of the commission’s positions on each of the amendments see annex: position of the commisssion.

in writing. - (el) we had no objections and voted in favour of the report (concerning portugal) but we would like to take this opportunity to point out that greece has been wronged in the allocation of milk quotas. the commission should table a proposal soon to correct this by doubling greece's cow milk quotas. the production quota of about 700 000 tonnes set for greece does not even cover the national demand of over 1 100 000 tonnes and when this quota is exceeded, livestock farmers have to spend a fortune on coresponsibility levies. at the same time, some dairy companies are known to have made huge profits at the expense of the cattle farmers - who are forced to sell their milk at ever lower prices - and to have raised the prices of the products purchased by the consumer. the livestock sector in greece is on the verge of disappearing and greek consumers are forced to buy imported products with a long shelf life because it takes too long to import fresh milk from the member states of the european union; however, greece has the potential to increase its production to meet the demand for fresh milk. we are asking for a fair distribution of quotas based on population figures, consumer demand and production potential.
in writing. - (el) our position on culture depends on the type of politics it promotes and the values, principles and aesthetic standards it puts forward. the main question is whether cultural activities exist for the benefit of workers and contribute to social progress or whether they exist for the benefit of large companies, extending their control and increasing their profits. the report expresses the need to integrate the cultural processes linked to the performing arts into the policies of the european union ideologically, politically and socially. the report calls for theatre to serve the 'european ideal' and to implement policies such as mobility and lifelong learning. it is aimed at the mediterranean countries and the countries of central and eastern europe in particular. but what is this "ideal" that we are asked to support? if this is not clear, how can we be expected to support contradictory procedures that solve some temporary problems (intellectual property rights, education, mobility) but also show that these problems have been caused by the intervention of big business in the culture of each country? we also disagree with the dominant role of corporations and private employers and with the vague definition of the emerging 'european cultural identity' and the methods used to impose it. that is why the deputies of the greek communist party abstained from voting.

commission. – as parliament knows, in december 2000 the commission proposed the framework decision on combating the sexual exploitation of children and child pornography. i am very happy to tell you that, on 14 october, the justice and home affairs council reached political agreement on the commission's proposal. this framework decision is one of the most far-reaching instruments so far when it comes to the approximation of criminal law. it addresses various forms of sexual exploitation of children, such as coercing a child into prostitution, pornographic performances and abuse of a recognised position of trust. child pornography, both on- and offline, is also covered. as regards penalties, the council has taken steps to establish a general approach to the approximation of penalties. this is also the main reason why the council took so long to adopt this framework decision. now, the framework decision stipulates levels of penalties ranging from between one and three years for basic offences to between five and ten years in circumstances that aggravate the offence. the framework decision does not approximate the age of sexual consent. the commission believes that this is an issue for the member states, in accordance with the principle of subsidiarity. the age of sexual consent, as defined by national law, is, however, used as a dividing line for different levels of penalties. the idea is to reflect the principle that the younger the victim the worse the offence. in terms of final assistance, the stop programme is a very valuable tool in combating paedophilia. the daphne programme also specifically refers to paedophilia. the commission will continue to support projects in this field through the new agis programme for law enforcement and judicial cooperation. turning to the final part of the question, the commission can confirm that it has comparative information on the legislative and judicial means to combat this tragic phenomenon. europol has produced a report on this subject, demonstrating that it is very active in combating child pornography. europol has recently been involved in several successful large-scale operations. the commission is convinced that once eurojust is fully operational, it will encourage and improve cooperation between the member states in the fight against the sexual exploitation of children, by facilitating mutual legal assistance and the implementation of extradition requests.
i am very grateful to you, commissioner, for the explanations you have given me. i believe that all colleagues share the importance of this issue and, above all, the particularity that the victims are children and are usually treated by the judicial and public authorities without the special seriousness, but also that they themselves cannot easily confess what they are suffering. so few cases go to court, and few cases are won over by the children. the phenomenon is becoming particularly acute because of enlargement, because not only are the frontiers opened up, facilitating crime, but at the root of the phenomenon is poverty, which is difficult to combat in the enlargement countries. will you take special measures to involve the enlargement countries in the response and cooperation programmes and in the care of the victims?
commission. – the european union is founded on the principles of liberty, democracy and respect for human rights and fundamental freedoms and the rule of law, principles which are common to the member states. they have been recognised by article 6(2) of the treaty on european union and reflected in the charter of fundamental rights. the commission is therefore unaware of any legal arrangements governing political prisoners in the democracies of the european union, since the mere notion of political prisoners, that is, people who have been in prison or prosecuted only for political reasons or opinions, would be against those founding principles of the union. as regards extradition between member states, therefore, mutual confidence in the national judicial systems has resulted in revision of the political offence derogation in relation to terrorist crimes. article 5(1) of the 1996 convention relating to extradition between the member states of the european union, which supplements the european convention on extradition of 13 december 1957 thus states: 'for the purposes of applying this convention, no offence may be regarded by the requested member state as a political offence, as an offence connected with a political offence or an offence inspired by political motives.' this statement covers the terrorist offences referred to in the first and second articles of the 1977 european convention on the suppression of terrorism, to which all member states of the union are party. the council framework decision of 13 june 2002 on the european arrest warrant, which will soon replace the previous extradition system, includes terrorism among the offences that will give rise to surrender, pursuant to a european arrest warrant, provided the offence is punishable in the issuing member state by a custodial sentence or detention order for a maximum period of at least three years. the political nature of the offence cannot be put forward as grounds for non-execution. finally, the council framework decision of 13 june 2002 on combating terrorism establishes a common definition of terrorist offences. it lists serious crimes deemed to be terrorist offences, when committed with the aim of seriously intimidating a population and unduly compelling a government or international organisation to perform or abstain from performing any act, or seriously destabilising or destroying the fundamental political, constitutional, economic or social structures of a country or an international organisation. in this definition, reference to ideological motives is therefore irrelevant, both for defining a terrorist offence or avoiding considering it as such.
i am not sure that this is the case. i will inform my colleague who is unable to be here today to discuss with you that no criminal act can be absolved of guilt, as my colleague points out in his preamble, because of ideological motivation.

president of the commission. mr president, mr president-in-office, ladies and gentlemen, i shall take a few minutes to answer a few questions which i consider very important: i am also very pleased to see this very broad support for the enlargement process, which has really united almost the whole of parliament in what i consider by far the most important political operation of my entire mandate the appeal to the will of the people in the enlargement process was not perfect, as in any political event, but it was the most extraordinary event, the one that struck me the most: this new commitment to the adoption of democratic principles, to solidarity, to respect for minorities, has transformed the world of the west. it was an extraordinary experience for me to see, in the parliaments of those countries, a continuous commitment of the parties, amongst themselves perhaps in complete disagreement on other issues but in agreement on enlargement. i'm sorry i don't share that very basic point. enlargement has been experienced as an ethical and political problem, not just an economic one. in fact, in many cases the economic aspect has been discussed, but this great sense of turning the pages of our history, this great sense of having unified europe - i repeat, not reunified but unified europe, because europe has never been united - is an extraordinarily strong achievement. and we have this - i always say this, i learned this in the parliament of one of the countries that will join our union - unified with a different criterion, so we can attract even smaller countries, we can also attract minorities, because ours is a union of minorities, it is a union in which no one commands: this is the new, absolute greatness of europe. mr poettering and mr barón crespo emphasized two major points: first, the prospect of taking the final step on the financial framework. there's not much to do, we can't play, for 3 euros each, this great historical effort. i have just read the figure for the cohesion chapter which the member states receive in relation to the new countries: this difference is explained by the fact that life is complicated, we have tight budgets, but it is not a difference that satisfies us, that satisfies our sense of solidarity. the same sense of solidarity, moreover, must be clearly borne in mind, as mr bigliardo has asked, for the future, for those in objective 1. we did this not by doing a quick math on the entry of new member states but, once again, by solving the problem with political sense and wisdom. my used. we are making this communication in cooperation with the member states and above all by using and supporting the structures of civil society at the service of our action. but this campaign the commission is not prepared to accept this. i would like to answer briefly the questions which have been put to me directly. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. i would like to make it very clear once again that we have a duty to write regular reports only on the future member states. i do not know what would happen if we were to write regular reports on the member states using the same standards and the same instruments. do you think we'd come to the conclusion that there's no corruption anywhere? do you think we'll come to the conclusion that privatization processes are transparent everywhere? do you think we'd come to the conclusion that there's no problem with the impact of media concentration on freedom of speech? or do you think that i could not show you in any major city in any member state a neighbourhood where people live in the same social declassification as in a roma settlement in slovakia, bulgaria or romania? i am simply saying that there is no reason for arrogance on our part when we look at the social reality here. just because there are no progress reports on the member states does not mean that they have to be much better in all respects than the countries that are now seeking to join. i think that we should be a little more open-minded on this question and try to be fair. i do not think it would be possible for a member of the commission to ask the head of government of a member state to go with him to the worst social and political hot spots and to declare publicly to the cameras of his country that he is ashamed of these conditions and that he will put an end to them. i have serious doubts whether there is any head of government of a member state who would do that to me. (applause from the centre) as regards the monitoring of safeguards, there are a wide range of monitoring instruments, and all of them are still being applied. the decisive factor is, of course, the final monitoring report six months before accession. the political pressure from this report alone will be so strong that we can really count on the fact that by then the remaining deficits will have been eliminated. the safeguards are as follows: i have told both the council and the dutch government last week that the new safeguard instrument we are proposing must be limited in its duration. the normal instruments of the treaty must be used again, not exceptional provisions. the two-year period proposed by the commission does not, however, fully exploit the possibilities that exist legally. i have put it in rather complicated terms, in other words: there is still a certain margin of manoeuvre here in terms of duration, and the commission does not object to the council making use of that margin of manoeuvre. i would like to say very emphatically that the whole instrument is useless if it is a matter of complicated procedures and we need, for example, unanimous decisions in the council to be able to apply it. then we can forget about it. (applause from the centre) then we won't need it. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. so if a slaughterhouse somewhere in a country is producing below standard, then we must be able to close it the next day. the next day and not after the council has unanimously decided to do so! it is a matter of such simple and practical questions, and the citizens of europe will not be reassured to hear that such an instrument can only be applied after months of procedure. i would like to make this very clear: it is only useful if it can be applied quickly and decisively. in terms of scope, the term 'internal market' refers to anything that has cross-border economic effects or to anything that has an impact on competition. i have doubts - the honourable member has asked whether environmental and social issues could not be included here - as to how this could be justified. if we see that the environmental acquis is not being fully implemented somewhere, i do not know why we should have the instrument of a rapid injunction in the candidate countries and the normal infringement procedure in the member states. we already have thousands of breach of contract lawsuits every year. it is not that the problem of non-transposition of the acquis does not exist in the eu today. i would therefore have difficulty in this, because i think we should actually restrict this instrument to the single market. moreover, safeguard clauses exist in all kinds of european union policies, such as food safety. these existing safeguards continue to exist and will of course be applied. the commission has already made a number of proposals. what concerns me very much about this whole discussion is the fact that the russian side has so far not been willing to talk about the real problem. i do not think that the issue of access, visas and transit is the real problem and i fully agree with what mr brok and others have said: when you look at the situation at this border, you cannot really believe that it is really about facilitating access. i think the real problem is to pursue a policy which prevents kaliningrad from falling behind socially and economically in a region which is developing very rapidly - and this region will develop very rapidly - and from which economic and, ultimately, political tensions must necessarily develop. how political tensions can be unleashed in such a geographical situation is something that everyone in this room can only imagine. this is precisely what must be prevented by engaging in a dialogue with russia on how to share the benefits of european integration throughout the baltic region with kaliningrad. i hope that when the vexing question of visas and transit is settled in a few days - as i believe it will be - then the way will be clear to discuss this other question, which is so important for the future of europe. in writing. - (el) it is unthinkable that the european union should draw up a constitution without first drawing up a declaration of rights, which is why the charter of fundamental rights should be given constitutional status and incorporated into the constitutional texts of the european union by means of a revision of the eu treaties before the accession of new members. this will give the charter a legal dimension and allow it to be cited in the european court of justice and in national courts. i share the view of the committee on constitutional affairs on this point, but i do not agree with the rapporteur's view that the charter should remain unchanged. on the contrary, it must be developed and extended in scope, since it lags far behind virtually all european constitutions in supporting fundamental rights.
in writing. - (el) two years ago, in our debates on the charter of fundamental rights, we called it useless and dangerous and stressed that it was lagging behind certain international conventions and the constitutions of several eu member states. we were afraid that it would be the centrepiece of an imminent community constitution which would pave the way for federalism. our fears are confirmed. the eu is becoming more and more autocratic, thanks in part to terrorism. it is updating its arsenal (european law on terrorism, european arrest warrant, european army, european police force) so that it can manage reactions to its policy. as for the social model, the workers know all about it: jobs are being cut, unemployment is being distributed, the social security and pension systems are being dismantled, and health and education are being privatised. the supporters of the charter, including the rapporteur who thinks that it needs no correction, have totally ignored the hundreds of thousands of demonstrators who, at each summit, have protested against everything that is anti-people and against the pro-monopoly system that is currently in place. they only want to press for the charter to be incorporated into the treaties and made binding on the eu institutions and the member states. if they succeed, we will have an even more conservative and autocratic institutional framework that will render the constitutionally protected rights acquired by workers useless. that is why the members of the european parliament from the greek communist party voted against this report.

the council. - mr president, i would like to answer the three questions in their entirety. and i would first of all stress that the council conclusions on the international criminal court do not encourage the signing of bilateral non-extradition agreements. it stressed that existing agreements between the united states and individual member states should be taken into account. we believe that many of the american concerns can be resolved on the basis of the existing agreements. it will be up to each member state to assess whether its bilateral or multilateral treaty relations with the united states can be considered adequate to meet american wishes. should a member state find it necessary to amend existing agreements or conclude a new agreement with the united states, the eu guiding principles will be taken into account. if the individual states remain within these outlined limits in their bilateral relations with the united states, the court will not be undermined. we are also making it quite clear that it would be incompatible with the obligations of states parties to the international criminal court under the statute of the court of justice to conclude agreements such as those now available from the united states. in accordance with the eu guiding principles, any solution should include appropriate operational provisions to ensure that persons who have committed crimes falling within the jurisdiction of the international criminal court do not enjoy impunity. they must therefore be prosecuted and, if necessary, punished. such provisions should ensure appropriate investigations and the prosecution of persons by the national authorities, if there is sufficient evidence, at the request of the court of justice. this guiding principle ensures that individuals are held accountable if they have committed war crimes, crimes against humanity or genocide. and they will be brought to justice, whether they are americans or europeans. the eu and the us agree that there should be individual responsibility for the most serious crimes of concern to the international community. the united states, in close cooperation with the eu, has for many years taken a leading role in the establishment of international criminal tribunals for the prosecution of such crimes. another guiding principle is that new agreements should only concern persons who are not nationals of a state party to the court of justice. and the eu has defined precisely which circles of people can be included. however, it is stated that certain persons are not subject to the jurisdiction of the court because they enjoy immunity from international law, but, apart from this, any solution should only cover persons who are present in the state requested to surrender a person to the court because they are sent there by the sending state. this is in line with the very narrow definition of the rome statute of persons who should be covered by non-extradition agreements. in view of the alternatives, the eu approach we have just described is the best way to defend the international criminal court. any other solution would have undermined the strong eu support for the icc and would have weakened it. a simple 'no' to the us proposal would, on the other hand, have had very negative consequences for transatlantic relations and would have jeopardized the vital american involvement in peacekeeping operations worldwide. the failure to adopt a common position of the union would have caused irreparable damage to the court. in this connection, i would like to mention that the united states has already concluded bilateral agreements with 12 countries which do not have the possibility of referring to the eu's position. if eu member states and others were to be referred to bilateral negotiations with the us without a strong eu consensus and without clear criteria for who should be covered by such bilateral agreements, a multiplicity of bilateral agreements would be obtained, which would certainly undermine the court of justice. as regards the views of eu citizens, ngos and legal experts, i would like to stress that the council did not discuss this issue and that the council did not reach the result it has reached in a vacuum. the council's conclusions are based on extensive consultations between the member states and on valuable contributions from the council's legal services and from the commission. the council also took account of the views expressed by the ngos concerned. throughout the process, contacts have been established with third countries which are parties to the court of justice and the presidency has also held exploratory talks with the united states. it goes without saying that the council has also taken full account of the views expressed by the elected representatives of the european union, both in the national parliaments and in the european parliament. as for the member states which might be willing to conclude non-surrender agreements with the united states, i am unfortunately not in a position at the moment to give any information, but i would like to answer questions concerning romania's bilateral agreement with the united states. it was signed on 1 august 2002. i would like to draw attention to the common position adopted on 11 june last year, which was amended on 20 june this year. this was a position supported by the candidate countries. the council has kept in close contact with the applicant countries throughout the preparation of the position and intends to continue these contacts in order to keep the applicant countries fully informed of further developments. the council noted that romania has not yet ratified the agreement in question and hoped that romania would take account of the position which the eu has established following the conclusion of the agreement.

the council. - mr president, i apologize for almost entering into a moment here, because the council does not comment on statements in the media either, but i can confirm that there is a europol document on the subject we are discussing. this document describes best practices for the treatment of informants in order to assist the police authorities of the member states, is non-binding and has been drawn up on the basis of article 3 (2) (i) of the europol convention. it is based, inter alia, on the decision of the executive committee of 29 april 1999 on general principles for the remuneration of whistleblowers and on the decision of the central group of 22 march 1999 on general principles for the remuneration of whistleblowers. they are thus integrated into the legal basis of the union, as provided for in the protocol integrating the schengen acquis into the legal basis of the union, both of which were published in the official journal of the european communities of 22 september 2000. the use of informants by the police, including issues of remuneration and immunity, naturally depends on the national legal situation in the member state concerned.

one dollar a day have been halved. this objective is being undermined by the cap revisions which reduce community agricultural production to adapt it to the conditions of the international market, as it is shaped by the wto, and not to the food needs of mankind. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a welcome step towards the creation of a european food safety net. with the continuous revision of the cap, the european union is becoming more and more short of products, is destroying its farmers and at the same time is forcing developing countries, through predatory agreements and price mechanisms, to sell their agricultural products at humiliating prices, tightening the noose on their peoples even more, securing profits for multinationals. the solution is not to abolish subsidies to developed countries so that their farmers become impoverished and eventually converge with those of developing countries, but to secure in practice equal international trade relations between developed and developing countries in order to improve the living standards of their peoples, reduce their external debt and increase their total domestic production. the report's wishful thinking and contradictions reach the height of hypocrisy with the proposal to use genetically modified organisms in the agriculture of developing countries, which will be supplied by the various multinationals, with whatever consequences. experience has shown that the problem of hunger is political and social, not dominance of capitalism, do not allow this. the report we are discussing is a step in the right direction and does not add anything positive to the alleviation of the world food problem. instead, it proposes measures that exacerbate the problems, adding serious risks to the environment, public health and the dependence of developing countries on developed-country multinationals.
the recommendation for second reading on a directive on market abuse, which we are considering today, is both timely and appropriate. it is timely because confidence in the integrity of market participants has been seriously damaged over the last two years as a result of various cases of market abuse, chiefly in the united states. it is appropriate because the common position addresses the need for regulatory authorities to have sufficient enforcement flexibility to be able to detect market abuse and sanction its practitioners on a consistent and effective basis. also covered is the right of market participants to enjoy reasonable legal certainty that their actions will be considered appropriate. the key is to be able to deal with market abuse in a consistent manner. those who have intent and knowledge of market abuse – and knowledge includes journalists – or who act recklessly will be punished. those whose actions are innocent but have unintended effects will not, however, be penalised. the fact that we now have a debate with so few amendments tabled nearly 18 months after the initial proposal, when the financial services industry initially questioned whether there was any need for such a proposal, owes much to the skill and patience of our rapporteur, mr goebbels. i would like to congratulate him. we have obtained an appropriate definition of market manipulation and of financial instruments whose use falls within the scope of the directive. nevertheless, member states must increase the resources available to their regulators so that they can be effective in their investigation and pursuit of market manipulators.

deputy rapporteur for the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy. mr president, the rapporteur, mrs malliori, is unable to attend because of fog at strasbourg airport and has asked me, with your permission, to make her views known on her behalf. mr president, every second a death is directly linked to smoking, which means more than 3 million victims on the planet every year. of these deaths, 500 000 are european citizens. 42% of the community population are smokers. the age at which smoking begins is steadily decreasing. 60% of smokers start smoking before they are 13 and 90% before they are 18. the gender gap in smoking is continually narrowing to the detriment of girls, and 500 000 children are born prematurely and with lower birth weight because their mothers are smokers. in homes where both parents smoke, children are at 72 percent greater risk of respiratory diseases, such as asthma. these are some of the reasons why the legislators of the member states of the european union felt obliged to restrict the right to advertise tobacco products in order to protect the public interest, namely the health of european citizens and especially young people. the world health organization's framework convention, which is currently being negotiated and will be concluded in the coming months, is moving in the same direction, and it emphasizes that the ban on tobacco advertising and sponsorship is one of the most urgent political measures to be implemented internationally. ladies and gentlemen, you are aware that the commission's proposal replaces directive 43, which, after ten years of negotiation, was finally voted by the european parliament and adopted by the council in 1998, but which was unfortunately annulled by the european court of justice in october 2000. the directive under discussion today, a year and a half after the commission's proposal, seeks to harmonise in part the different laws, regulations and administrative provisions of the member states in order to remove obstacles to the functioning of the internal market, with a view to ensuring the free movement of goods and services which comply with the rules of the directive. in this context, taking into account the current legislation of the member states and the case law of the european court of justice, this directive adopts parts of the preceding directive and prohibits advertising in printed media, on the internet, on radio and sponsorship of events with cross-border impact. it also prohibits the free distribution of tobacco products but does not cover indirect advertising. it is not based, as the previous one was, on the principle of a complete ban on all forms of advertising and sponsorship. it should be noted, however, that the commission submitted this limited proposal in the light of the legal framework established by the court of justice, but also submitted a proposal for a recommendation containing elements which, as i have said, are not included in this directive. unfortunately, mr president, the committee on legal affairs has adopted 25 amendments which, on the one hand, create the possibility of a new appeal to the european court of justice and, on the other hand, further restrict the scope of the directive. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the environmental impact of the use of pesticides is not reduced. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very important one. i therefore ask you to vote against all the amendments tabled by the committee on legal affairs. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work is a step in the right direction, and it is a step in the right direction.

from these experiences. the proposed directive seeks to protect public health by invoking the legal basis of non-distortion of competition. this hermaphroditic situation must be borne in mind by those who advocate more effective health protection against the spread of tobacco use, which unfortunately has no foundation in the treaty. the new proposal is therefore likely to be more moderate in terms of health protection than the cancelled proposal, which also covered indirect advertising. the committee on legal affairs of the european parliament has considered that the cross-border nature of the ban, not only on sponsorship but also on the press, is necessary in order to strengthen its validity. by definition, advertising and sponsorship at national level have no impact on the internal market. the legal basis chosen allows the regulation of advertising only for advertising media which are of relevance to the internal market. as regards forms circulated exclusively within the territory of a member state, they therefore have no cross-border impact and cannot cause obstacles to the functioning of the internal market or serious distortions of competition. this is also apparent from the proposed regulation on the sponsorship of events and from the fact that the proposal for a directive makes no reference to outdoor advertising of tobacco products and to advertising in writing. - (el) the situation is getting worse, especially with regard to electronic means of communication. tendency-driven information, censorship or distortion of news and views 'incompatible' with the dominant interests, general bottom-up approach and total disregard for workers' rights are commonplace in most modern media. the main reason is that information and news are in the hands of a few large shareholders, who are also powerful companies. their aim is to control information and impose an ideology, a way of life and consumption habits. big business has aggressively taken over areas that belong to the state, with its blessing, of course. all we can do is suggest some improvements, such as: banning media owners (even single shareholders) from owning shares in companies bidding for public works or contracts; insist on the registration of the shares of these companies; ensuring that no one owns more than one media of the same type; finally, the debate on state radio and television stations subject to permanent and effective public control should be re-launched. the resolution under debate is blind to the real dangers and is limited to a general position on pluralism and freedom of expression and to incomprehensible proposals (obviously the result of compromise) on whether and how the european union should legislate in this area; the european parliament has voted against the resolution.
in writing. - (el) i voted in favour of the brok report on enlargement, because i believe that the plenary must send a positive signal to the 10 countries applying for membership of the european union. however, i have serious reservations about certain aspects of the resolution. i think that the european parliament must get rid of the present financial carcan and demand a substantial increase in the community budget so that the countries benefiting from the cohesion fund can continue to receive structural aid and that new members are not treated as second-class citizens financially. it also believes that our main priority must be to support the broad sectors of european society that have been marginalized by neo-liberal policies, the cheap sale of public goods and the dismantling of social protection systems.

in writing. - (el) the collapse of socialism in central and eastern europe had disastrous consequences for research, as it did for the social achievements of the socialist era. funding levels have fallen to the lowest levels, research institutes have closed their doors, researchers have abandoned their work, new researchers have not been encouraged and - worst of all - there has been a brain drain, with the most experienced researchers leaving for the west. former socialist countries with advanced research facilities have been reduced to living off the miserable alms of foreign and eu aid. the report recommends that the candidate countries join the eu's research framework agreement, which it raises to the status of a panacea. at the same time, however, it advises linking research with industry and creating a favourable climate for trade; in other words, it is pushing for the adoption of the european model and wants research to be carried out in accordance with the rules of the market. while there is nothing to object to international scientific cooperation operating on an equal basis, we cannot under any circumstances allow research to be controlled and served by major financial interests. for these reasons, the meps from the greek communist party did not support the report.
in writing. - (el) i voted in favour of the amended version of the proposal which prohibits advertising for tobacco products because all the amendments tabled by the legal affairs committee were rejected. these amendments, first, weakened the restrictions contained in the proposal; second, allowed advertising companies to circumvent these restrictions; thirdly, there was uncertainty as to the legal basis, particularly after the court of justice annulled the first proposal. however, this second commission proposal is equally uncertain, since it is based on the internal market rather than on the protection of public health.
in writing. - (el) first of all, we think that the motion for a resolution on the integration of environmental protection requirements into the common fisheries policy is a good idea, and we support it. the proposal rightly notes that the european parliament is opposed to the horizontal application of restrictive measures, that is to say measures which do not take account of the specific characteristics of each fishing area or the specificities of the fish species. in doing so, the european parliament indirectly takes a stand against the basic option of reforming the common fisheries policy, which would mean the end of public aid for the modernisation of fishing vessels. the proposal also correctly identifies the dangers to the economies of the coastal areas affected by these measures. the proposal rightly notes that the commission's arguments in favour of the application of restrictive policies are not based on scientific data and that specialised studies will have to be carried out in order to assess the real situation of fish stocks. the proposal provides for both the carrying out of these studies and the maintenance of restrictions such as tacs and quotas. we would point out that it is contradictory to propose these restrictive measures without first having completed the studies mentioned above and that we have reservations about any further strengthening of the role of the commission in helping to strengthen integration, which we oppose.
mr president, i would like first of all to congratulate the rapporteur, mrs van den burg, for the excellent work she has done in preparing this report on the future of financial regulation in europe, which is such a complicated and controversial subject. i would like to emphasise that after the global economic downturn which started through the collapse of the new technology market, what was stunningly shown in the united states was the danger of laisser faire regulation in a booming market. would the financial accounting scandals have occurred had regulation been as intensive as it is now? i wager it would not. of course this question can be countered by asking whether it would have been possible to introduce such strict regulations in a booming market. a booming market has been known to act as a great catalyst to taking liberties with the law and even with honesty. it is perhaps one advantage of the market slump that we have at the moment that we can use this opportunity to introduce the kind of regulation that will stand us in good stead when euphoria takes over in the financial markets again, as it inevitably will. one can ask whether the down-turn would have been as steep had we not had wave after wave of revelations about the lack of internal controls and external oversight. again, one would say no, but now is the time to strengthen the rules for when the good times return, in a reversal of the fat cows, lean cows story. i am not arguing in favour of heavy-handed regulation nor for regulation that goes against the spirit of the internal market or the financial services action plan. i am quite sure that the vast majority of parliamentarians are in favour of creating a single market in financial services in europe, as we have shown over the past couple of years in the various positions adopted on financial services action plan legislation. i do not share some of my colleagues' compunctions about a european regulator. i do not see that the securities and exchange commission has done any harm in the united states. there is much evidence, including the gyllenhammar report, supporting the benefits of a unified financial market and showing that the integration of the financial services market in europe would bring substantial economic benefits. that is only to be expected. anything that would prop up the investment process in our economy is bound to bring financial benefits because we are so weak in this area. according to the report, gains could be in the region of at least half a percentage point per annum in additional economic growth. one could also call upon the evidence of the results of the new survey published only last week by the commission, which suggested that total european employment could increase by half a percentage point over the next decade as a result of financial markets integration. one must accept that increased integration increases the responsibilities of the european institutions to actually discover the best form of regulation, to consult as thoroughly as possible, to implement and interpret legislation as consistently as possible and, when problems arise, to be flexible. so far, i fear that under the new lamfalussy procedures there is not sufficient evidence that the commission has been operating according to these consultation principles. lack of initial consultation over the draft market abuse directive and last minute changes to the draft of the investment services directive are not good signs. i would argue, therefore, that we must be very cautious in extending the lamfalussy procedures to other financial services fields. we must have time and the proper analysis of how the process has been functioning. the institutional monitoring group on the lamfalussy procedure has not yet produced their first report. under these circumstances i am against any hurried extension of the lamfalussy procedure.
mr president, the directive on temporary work through the appropriate companies marks an even more organized and methodical attack on the right to full and stable employment and social security. it shows that the policy of flexible forms of employment is ruthless, intensifies the exploitation of workers and disorganizes the struggle of the labour movement. the fact that this is done with the agreement of the compromised leaderships of the trade union movement and the international labour organization merely reveals their disastrous role. it is a fraud to claim that the directive safeguards and improves the occupational position of the worker who is a victim, that it improves safety and health and that it increases employment. the truth is that it is a well-organized effort to intensify exploitation and share unemployment through the creation of modern slave-trading offices. the safeguarding of full, stable employment with rights for all is always a vital objective, which can only be achieved by strengthening the fight against monopoly capital and the european union's anti-working class policy.

in writing. - (el) we oppose direct or indirect advertising for tobacco and support measures to counteract the spread and increase of this unhealthy habit, especially among young people. however, let us stress once again that an anti-smoking campaign is not the same as an anti-tobacco campaign, and that anti-tobacco campaigns should not take the form of a campaign against tobacco cultivation in the eu member states, as the eu argues in amendment no 17 to the report. to say that tobacco plantations are responsible and that tobacco growers should be punished by withdrawing subsidies is to deliberately confuse the issue of protecting public health with the maintenance of traditional monoculture farms, which provide the livelihood for tens of thousands of farmers, particularly in the poor southern regions, who are not offered any specific alternative. these proposals are hypocritical and run counter to the interests of consumers and public health, since 70% of the tobacco in the european union is imported. thus, removing aid to community production will not reduce smoking; this will only make us dependent on imports and flood the european market with tobacco from third countries.
in writing. - (el) first of all, i agree that there should be controls and rules for the herbal medicine market, which is a total mess that allows charlatans to profit at the expense of public health and consumers. however, the question that is troubling us is how herbal medicines can reach patients and consumers. the report explains that these drugs will be available over-the-counter, leaving the entire responsibility for choosing a suitable product to the patient or consumer, whose only source of information will be the manufacturer's advertising. in other words, consumers will be at the mercy of misleading information from the private sector and large industrial groups, who can reap lucrative profits without any worries. the second question is whether these medicines should be sold in pharmacies and by qualified pharmacists or whether they should be available on supermarket shelves. it seems that the balance will be in favour of the second option. we believe that once these preparations are classified - and they should be classified - as medicines, they should be properly prescribed by a doctor or pharmacist, and should be available for health care.
in writing. - (el) from the progress made in recent years, it is clear that biotechnology can help us enormously in understanding how life develops, in fighting disease and in developing new medicines. but at the same time, biotechnology is fraught with dangers, especially when it comes to "playing" with genetic material, changing genetic codes, cloning, preparing genetically modified organisms (gmos), patenting living organisms and using dna to track people down. the commission and the european parliament have little interest in developing biotechnology for the benefit of society. the only thing they are interested in is how european monopolies can catch up with american monopolies, but that, of course, does not surprise us. the main battle in this war is over genetically modified food and we are racing the united states in this respect. who cares about the risks we take playing with life and making artificial changes? who cares about the unpredictable effects of these organisms on our planet's delicate ecosystem? the responsibility for prevention has been placed squarely on the shoulders of consumers (such as the millions of starving africans). that is why the members of the greek communist party voted against this report.

in writing. - (el) the proposed changes and the idea of coordinating general economic policy with the guidelines for employment policy could be a step in the right direction, contributing to a radical change in economic policy, if only the very serious social problem were a priority which other policies should take into account. however, the opposite is happening. the emphasis is still on the stability pact and the policy of privatisation, mergers and capitalist restructuring, which has led - and still leads - to massive redundancies, a reduction in living standards and wages, a scarcity of social services and violations of workers' rights. it is therefore to be feared that coordination will cause even more problems, as social issues are considered to be secondary to the policy of liberalization and the ever present principle of benefits. of course, coordination does not include two of the four pillars proclaimed by the eu, namely environmental protection and social cohesion, but it adds the completion of the single internal market. we believe that the only purpose of this kind of "coordination" is to mislead the workers. we are in favour of a policy based on the objective of stable full employment based on people and their needs, leading to the protection of the social rights of all workers, the raising of living standards and real social change and prosperity.
in writing. - (el) it is very clear from the vote in favour of an increasing number of obstacles to the fisheries sector that the sole aim of the eu is to establish a restrictive fisheries policy. previous policies have succeeded in reducing the number of vessels, especially coastal fishing vessels, hitting small family businesses hard and driving many people out of the profession. under the pretext of maintaining fish stocks, the aim is to end subsidies and limit funding for fleet renewal, thus accelerating the demise of small and medium-sized enterprises in countries such as greece, where fishing has traditionally been of both social and economic importance. the aim is to put the fisheries in the hands of large companies. this will destroy the social aspect of fishing, which will become the preserve of a few fishermen employed by fishing companies, who will be cut off from their social roots. at the same time, the aim is to develop the aquaculture sector in the context of general increases in production of all types of aquaculture products. what we need is a change in general direction and approach, which is not in the nature of the capitalist eu, and a comprehensive policy based on the interests of small and mediumsized farming and fishing enterprises and not those of large companies.

commission - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, by the force of things, being the last one to be entered, i shall therefore be the last to say something positive about mr bourlanges' report, but i shall not have been the only one. this report is remarkable in many ways. firstly, because it covers very broadly one of the fundamental points of the issues raised at laeken. secondly, by the rigour, efficiency and conciseness of the proposals which your assembly puts forward in this report, and which in fact lead to a genuine simplification of our regulatory system, which the rapporteur, mr bourlanges, himself said resembled a kind of chaos. finally, let me say this through the very rich, skilful and clever pen of your rapporteur, whose talent daily contradicts what was once said by a great french diplomat, mr bourlanges, who knows him well and whose name is talleyrand. he falsely claimed that the mind serves all purposes but leads nowhere. on the contrary, mr rapporteur, your report is very clever in its approach to a very well-argued resolution. i would like to express the commission's full agreement with the main conclusions of this resolution, in accordance with our own communication, the one we prepared with president prodi and my colleague mr vitorino here on 5 december on institutional architecture. i would like to stress three points. the first concerns the very principle of a legal organisation of the union in three blocks - constitutional, legislative and regulatory - which would bring clarity to the functioning of the union, which is sorely lacking today. this reorganisation is essential to clarify the role of each institution in the field of standards: responsibility for drawing up legislative standards lies with the european parliament and the council, while the commission has primary responsibility for enforcing legislative standards and adopting implementing standards. naturally, this is coupled with the importance of the procedures for adopting and revising future constitutional treaties, for which the commission, like your assembly, wishes to make more general use of the convention model. beyond procedural issues, parliament and the commission share the same view on the need to include sectoral policies in the constitutional treaty. we must not forget that we cannot have strong institutions with weak policies. the second point, ladies and gentlemen, which is essential in this report, concerns the creation of a genuine legislative bloc. the time has come, as you yourself have said, to call things by their proper names and therefore to talk about european laws. these laws shall be enacted by a legislative authority divided into two equal branches; the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this would finally provide a clear and transparent framework for all citizens, although it is clear that some aspects of the codecision procedure should be streamlined. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. my third point concerns the role you want the european commission to play in implementing legislation. i am not talking here about the functions of government as such, although i note in passing that the exercise of these functions must also be clarified, which must be shared between the commission, which proposes, and the council, which decides, with appropriate information from the european parliament. this applies to police and justice, and to the common foreign and security policy. but as far as the community's powers to implement european legislation are concerned, we want, like you, that they be clearly entrusted to the commission and controlled on an equal footing by parliament and the council, that is to say by both the legislative and budgetary branches of the authority. mrs randzio-plath has emphasized this point. this is a key rationalization of who is doing what in the european union and to introduce more transparency and accountability in the exercise of these implementing powers. in this connection, ladies and gentlemen, i should like to express the commission's concern about two points in your report. the first was mentioned earlier by mrs frassoni. this is the possibility of conferring, at least in part, these implementing powers on an authority other than the commission. ladies and gentlemen, let us beware of encouraging or facilitating what might seem to be a dismantling of the regulatory power in the european union. the second point of concern, which has been raised by mr corbett and mrs pervenche berès and others, is the granting of a general right of appeal to parliament and the council on implementing measures. i'm talking about the execution measures. this point is currently the subject of a reasoned discussion mutatis mutandis in the context of the preparation of the draft interinstitutional agreement on better legislation and my colleague, mrs de palacio, will raise it here tomorrow. i shall not go back to the commission's position, which is well known. let me simply say that each institution must be able to exercise its prerogatives in this area of implementing measures autonomously, in balance with the other institutions, and without placing citizens and economic entities at risk of legal uncertainty. mr president, in conclusion, i would like to congratulate the members of the committee on constitutional affairs and your rapporteur very sincerely on the inspired work they have done with this report. we will have to fight to impose the ambitious conclusions that emerge from it. there is a certain distance between the bourlanges report, as you are able to adopt it, and the conclusions of the working party headed by mr amato in the convention. to close this gap in the drafting of the future constitutional treaty, it is essential that the commission and your parliament continue to work in the same direction within the convention. you can count on mr. vitorino and myself to do that. this is indeed an issue that mrs kaufmann and mr duff have raised earlier and which makes mr bourlanges' report all the more useful and timely. - the next item is the recommendation for second reading (a5-0404/2002) by mrs damião, on behalf of the committee on employment and social affairs, on the common position adopted by the council with a view to adopting a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos at work.
mr president, while asbestos has been banned in 13 countries and largely phased out because it is a proven carcinogen, greece, by implementing community legislation allowing the use of asbestos until 2005, continues to put the lives and health of both workers in the sector and the social sector at risk. it is clear that the protection of the environment and public health are not among the priorities of the european union, despite its declarations. the priority is the pursuit of maximum profit for big business. the asbestos scandal is a glaring example of the barbarity of the capitalist system. the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. at the same time, measures must be taken to protect public health from asbestos and to rehabilitate workers in the sector and compensate them for the damage to their health suffered over the years.
mr president, apart from the details given in the report on foot-and-mouth disease in great britain and other countries, we regard the emergence and spread of this disease as the natural consequence of a number of factors. the first factor is the complete freedom, or rather the lack of hygiene, in the way animals and products of animal origin are currently moved around the world as a result of globalisation, which is required by big business to make it easier to make and take profits. the second factor is the substantial abolition of crossborder veterinary checks on the movement of livestock between the member states of the european union. this abolition effectively makes the movement of animals from one country to another uncontrollable and thus contributes to the transmission of dangerous animal diseases. the third factor is the abolition of public veterinary services, the reduction of their staff and, in general, the undervaluing of the state control mechanisms. all this is due to the requirement of large capital to minimise controls either across borders or within countries, because controls can become an obstacle to the needs of fast moving livestock and the resulting profit. finally, not unrelated to the above developments is the factor of feeding animals foods that are completely uncontrolled, which seems to have led to the spread of foot-and-mouth disease. this factor is not insignificant, as has been shown by other crises within the european union in the livestock sector, such as dioxins in poultry and spongiform encephalopathies in cattle. we have no illusions that such crises threatening livestock and public health will continue to occur as long as the criterion of profit and the rules of production, circulation and marketing imposed by multinational and other capitalist enterprises in the sector dominate production. any structural and other measures taken will be of a piecemeal nature and will be effective until the next crisis occurs. the only long-term solution which will not threaten the agricultural economy of countries or destroy entire livestock enterprises is through the abolition of capitalist profit in production and the introduction of adequate border and other controls against the demands of multinational enterprises which have achieved their abolition.
in writing. - (el) the proposal concerns the court of auditors' report on the implementation of the socrates and youth for europe programmes (1995-1999). the court of auditors points out shortcomings in the design (the socrates programme is too complicated, leading to different interpretations and problems in the relations between the technical assistance office and the national agencies); in the implementation (delay, management failures, suspicion of fraud, referral of seven cases to olaf); and in evaluation. the delays and irregularities in the evaluation were such that 'the court even had difficulty deciding whether the objectives of the programmes had been achieved', while the mid-term report on the youth for europe programme was entrusted to a company owned by a former director of the bat. the proposal contains some positive comments and proposals. the commission is invited to follow the recommendations of the court of auditors, including the replacement of the bats by community public law agencies. similarly, any 'framework contracts' with external companies should be abolished and monitoring and evaluation tasks should be assigned to public bodies. some doubts also arise as to the effectiveness of the programmes themselves. however, positive annotations are simply measures to improve programmes which, as we have repeatedly pointed out, have been designed to serve objectives unrelated to their pompous titles. these are programmes which attempt to impose the 'euro-unionist' model of education on the education and youth sectors and to familiarize young people with the so-called 'european ideal'. in view of the above, we, the members of the greek communist party, abstain from voting.

today it's happening right next to us. we are observers, too. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect, and i am sure that the commission will be able to support them. this is not a complaint station. this is an intervention position. stop the settlements! they lead nowhere. stop the destruction! they lead nowhere. we must stop the acts of terrorism! they lead nowhere. the policy decided many years ago between arafat and the late rabin, the prime minister of israel, must continue! this is the way out, this is the solution. any other policy is pointless. in this sense, a european union of 25 which wants to play a political role and intervene in international affairs must change its approach to this issue. he can't be a bystander. he has to be a leading man and it's a chance now to play that part that he should have played many years ago. again, thank you for listening and merry christmas!
